Lemma 4.5
.

The operations
\Delta_{j}
,
\circ_{i,j}
and
\eta_{i,j}
are well defined cochain maps and hence induce morphisms on cohomology that we denote by the same letters.

Proof.

This is clear for
\Delta_{j}
, since the operadic composition is compatible with the differentials.

For
\mu_{i,j}
one has to check that

A:=d\mu_{i,j}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})-\mu_{i,j}(d\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})-(-1)^{|\Gamma|}\mu_{i,j}(\Gamma,d\Gamma^{\prime})=0.

The differential can be decomposed as
d=d_{c}+d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}
, with
d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}
the part induced from the differential on
D\mathsf{BV}^{*}
, and
d_{c}
the edge contraction.
The operation
\mu_{i,j}
is clearly compatible with the internal part of the differential
d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}
:

\displaystyle d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\mu_{i,j}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}(\mu_{i,j}(\Delta_{i}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})+\mu_{i,j}(\Gamma,\Delta_{j}\Gamma^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mu_{i,j}(d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Delta_{i}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})+(-1)^{|\Gamma|}\mu_{i,j}(\Delta_{i}\Gamma,d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\mu_{i,j}(d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Gamma,\Delta_{j}\Gamma^{\prime})+(-1)^{|\Gamma|}\mu_{i,j}(\Gamma,d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Delta_{j}\Gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mu_{i,j}(\Delta_{i}d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})+(-1)^{|\Gamma|}\mu_{i,j}(\Delta_{i}\Gamma,d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\mu_{i,j}(d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Gamma,\Delta_{j}\Gamma^{\prime})+(-1)^{|\Gamma|}\mu_{i,j}(\Gamma,\Delta_{j}d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mu_{i,j}(d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Gamma.\Gamma^{\prime})-(-1)^{|\Gamma|}\mu_{i,j}(\Gamma.d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}\Gamma^{\prime}).

In the analogous computation with
d_{c}
there appears a new term, from contracting the new edge introduced by
\mu_{i,j}
.
Denote by
\gamma\in D\mathsf{BV}^{*}
the decoration at the vertex connected to leg
i
in
\Gamma
and denote by
\gamma^{\prime}\in D\mathsf{BV}^{*}
the decoration at the vertex connected to leg
j
in
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Then the obstruction
A
to compatibility with the differential is a graph with one vertex decorated by

(\gamma\circ_{h}\mathbf{\Delta^{*}})\circ_{h^{\prime}}\gamma^{\prime}+\gamma\circ_{h}(\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}\circ_{h^{\prime}}\gamma^{\prime})=0,

with the terms cancelling since the composition “
\circ
” is an operation of odd degree.
This shows that
\circ_{i,j}
is a well-defined cochain map.

The proof of well-definedness of
\eta_{i,j}
is analogous to that of
\circ_{i,j}
.
∎

4.3.
The three operations in high weight

In this section we shall understand the three operations
\Delta_{j}
,
\circ_{i,j}
and
\eta_{i,j}
of the previous subsection in the case of high weights, that is, weights
6g-6+2n
and
6g-8+2n
.
Taking into account the computation of those high weight parts in Corollary
4.3
, there is the following list of nontrivial cases to consider:

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}:\mathrm{gr}_{2n-8}H^{2n-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n)\!))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\to\mathrm{gr}_{2n-6}H^{2n-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n)\!))

\displaystyle\circ_{i,j}:\mathrm{gr}_{2n-6}H^{2n-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n)\!))\otimes\mathrm{gr}_{2n^{\prime}-6}H^{2n^{\prime}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n^{\prime})\!))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\to\mathrm{gr}_{2n+2n^{\prime}-10}H^{2n+2n^{\prime}-10}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n+n^{\prime}-2)\!))

\displaystyle\circ_{i,j}:\mathrm{gr}_{2n-8}H^{2n-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n)\!))\otimes\mathrm{gr}_{2n^{\prime}-6}H^{2n^{\prime}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n^{\prime})\!))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\to\mathrm{gr}_{2n+2n^{\prime}-12}H^{2n+2n^{\prime}-12}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n+n^{\prime}-2)\!))

\displaystyle\circ_{i,j}:\mathrm{gr}_{2n-2}H^{2n-2}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(1,n)\!))\otimes\mathrm{gr}_{2n^{\prime}-6}H^{2n^{\prime}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n^{\prime})\!))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\to\mathrm{gr}_{2n+2n^{\prime}-6}H^{2n+2n^{\prime}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(1,n+n^{\prime}-2)\!))

\displaystyle\eta_{i,j}:\mathrm{gr}_{2n-8}H^{2n-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n)\!))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\to\mathrm{gr}_{2n-6}H^{2n-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(1,n-2)\!)).

Here we also used the symmetry of
\circ_{i,j}
to remove one case.

Proposition 4.6
.

The operation

\circ_{i,j}:\mathrm{gr}_{2n-6}H^{2n-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n)\!))\otimes\mathrm{gr}_{2n^{\prime}-6}H^{2n^{\prime}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n^{\prime})\!))\cong\mathsf{Com}(\!(n)\!)\otimes\mathsf{Com}(\!(n^{\prime})\!)\\
\to\mathrm{gr}_{2n+2n^{\prime}-10}H^{2n+2n^{\prime}-10}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n+n^{\prime}-2)\!))\cong\mathsf{Com}(\!(n+n^{\prime}-2)\!)

agrees with minus the cyclic operadic composition of the cyclic operad
\mathsf{Com}
, using the identification (
18
).

Proof.

We consider an arbitrary leg as the root, and define the corresponding non-cyclic sequences

(21)

\displaystyle Y(S):=\mathrm{gr}_{2r-4}H^{2r-4}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2})(\!(0,\{0\}\sqcup S)\!))\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2r-4}H^{2r-4}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(0,\{0\}\sqcup S)\!)),

with
S
running over finite sets and
r:=|S|
.
Then the above morphisms
\circ_{i,j}
are equivalent to morphisms

\hat{\circ}_{*}:Y(S\sqcup\{*\})\otimes Y(S^{\prime})\to Y(S\sqcup S^{\prime}).

We know that
Y(S)\cong\mathsf{Com}(S)\cong\mathbb{Q}
, and we want to show that
\circ_{j}
corresponds to the composition in the operad
\mathsf{Com}
, or in other words to the usual isomorphism
\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{Q}
.

From Section
4.1
and Example
3.6
we see that
Y(S)
is generated by the 1-vertex graph decorated with the element

\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{j\in S}x_{j})\in\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2}(S).

Let us denote the graph in
Y(S)
with one vertex decorated by
x\in\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2}(S)
by

T(x)=\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to40.9pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.42638pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}

and the two-vertex graph with vertices decorated by

.
We then compute

\displaystyle T\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i})\right)\hat{\circ}_{j}T\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}x_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle T_{S^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{s}u\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}x_{j})\right)-T_{S^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}u\nabla(\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}x_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle-d_{\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2}}\underbrace{\left(T_{S^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{s}x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}x_{j})\right)-T_{S^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}x_{j}\right)\right)}_{=:X}.

Using the notation (
11
) the result is hence cohomologous to

\displaystyle d_{c}X

\displaystyle=-T\left(\sum_{i<j\in S^{\prime}}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij}+\sum_{i<j\in S\sqcup S^{\prime}\atop i\text{ or }j\in S}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=-T\left(\sum_{i<j\in S\sqcup S^{\prime}}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij}\right)=-T\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla\prod_{i\in S\sqcup S^{\prime}}x_{i}\right).

Hence we have shown that the result is minus the standard generator of
Y(S\sqcup S^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Q}
, and we have thus shown the Proposition.

Also note that the notation “
i<j
” in the above computation is slightly abusive and shall indicate that we sum over each pair only once.
∎

Proposition 4.7
.

The operation

\circ_{i,j}:\mathrm{gr}_{2n-8}H^{2n-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n)\!))\otimes\mathrm{gr}_{2n^{\prime}-6}H^{2n^{\prime}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n^{\prime})\!))\\
\cong\mathsf{HyCom}(\!(n)\!)^{2}\otimes\mathsf{Com}(\!(n^{\prime})\!)\cong\mathsf{HyCom}(\!(n)\!)^{2}\otimes\mathsf{HyCom}(\!(n^{\prime})\!)^{0}\\
\to\mathrm{gr}_{2n+2n^{\prime}-8}H^{2n+2n^{\prime}-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n+n^{\prime}-2)\!))\cong\mathsf{HyCom}(\!(n)\!)^{2}

agrees with minus the cyclic operadic composition in
\mathsf{HyCom}
, using the identifications (
18
), (
19
).

Proof.

Again, we can show that statement in the non-cyclic setting, using the model
\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2}
instead of
D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*}
.
Representatives of

Z(S):=\mathrm{gr}_{2r-8}H^{2r-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathsf{BV}^{!}_{\geq 3})(\!(0,\{0\}\sqcup S)\!))\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2r-8}H^{2r-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,\{0\}\sqcup S)\!)),

with
S
a finite set of cardinality
r
,
have been computed in Section
4.1
. Using the notation therein, they are given by formal linear combinations of trees

\sum_{A\subset S\atop 2\leq|A|\leq n-1}c_{A}T_{A}

satisfying the condition
7
.
Comparing to Section
3.2
the symbol
T_{A}
corresponds to
\delta_{A}^{*}
there.

We need to consider the composition morphisms
\circ_{\blacklozenge}:=\circ_{\blacklozenge,0}

(23)

\displaystyle\circ_{\blacklozenge}\colon Z(S\sqcup\{\blacklozenge\})\otimes Y(S^{\prime})\to Z(S\sqcup S^{\prime})

(24)

\displaystyle\circ_{\blacklozenge}\colon Y(S^{\prime}\sqcup\{\blacklozenge\})\otimes Z(S)\to Z(S\sqcup S^{\prime}),

using the notation (
21
).
We have to check that these compositions adhere to the description
9
of the
\mathsf{Com}
-action on
\mathsf{HyCom}
.

\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2}

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 90.55 -43.31 M 90.55 -43.31 C 90.03 -44.35 88.82 -45.04 87.09 -45.04 L 82.2 -45.04 C 80.47 -45.04 79.26 -45.73 78.74 -46.77 C 78.22 -45.73 77.01 -45.04 75.28 -45.04 L 70.39 -45.04 C 68.66 -45.04 67.45 -44.35 66.93 -43.31}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{}}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{52.86212pt}{-43.25034pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

In particular, the elements
T_{A}
above are expressed through (
22
) as

T_{A}=T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right).

We first consider the composition (
23
) and compute the image of
\sum_{A}c_{A}T_{A}
under
\circ_{\blacklozenge}
.
This is

\displaystyle\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\in A}c_{A}T_{A\setminus\{\blacklozenge\},S^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}u\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle-~

\displaystyle\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\in A}c_{A}T_{A\setminus\{\blacklozenge\},S^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}u\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\notin A}c_{A}T_{A,S^{\prime}}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{s}u\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle-~

\displaystyle\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\notin A}c_{A}T_{A,S^{\prime}}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}u\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=-~

\displaystyle d_{\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2}}\left(\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\in A}c_{A}T_{A\setminus\{\blacklozenge\},S^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\prod_{i\in A}x_{i},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)\right.

\displaystyle-~

\displaystyle\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\in A}c_{A}T_{A\setminus\{\blacklozenge\},S^{\prime}}(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\notin A}c_{A}T_{A,S^{\prime}}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{s}x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle-~

\displaystyle\left.\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\notin A}c_{A}T_{A,S^{\prime}}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=:-~

\displaystyle d_{\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2}}(X),

where we abbreviated the last term in brackets by
X
.
The result is cohomologous to
d_{c}X
.
The edge contraction
d_{c}
can act on each tree occurring in
X
, and contract one of two edges.
Some of the terms yield a trivial contribution due to the closedness
d_{c}\sum c_{A}T_{A}=0
.
Concretely, contracting an edge between two vertices with degree 1 decorations yields a trivial contribution for that reason.
Next, the terms contracting an edge towards the
S^{\prime}
-vertex can be handled as in the proof of Proposition
4.6
above.
We obtain

\displaystyle d_{c}X=

\displaystyle\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\in A}c_{A}T_{S^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{i<j\in S\atop i\text{ or }j\in A^{c}}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)-\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\in A}c_{A}T_{A\sqcup S^{\prime}\setminus\{\blacklozenge\}}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A\sqcup S^{\prime}\setminus\{\blacklozenge\}}x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\notin A}c_{A}T_{S^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{i<j\in A}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)-\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\notin A}c_{A}T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in A^{c}\sqcup S^{\prime}\setminus\{\blacklozenge\}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)

The first and third term together have the form

\displaystyle\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\in A}c_{A}T_{S^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{i<j\in S\atop i\text{ or }j\in A^{c}}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)-\sum_{A\atop\blacklozenge\notin A}c_{A}T_{S^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{i<j\in A}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=T_{S^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{i<j\in S}\alpha_{ij}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in S^{\prime}}x_{i})\right)

with

(25)

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\atop{\blacklozenge\in A\atop i\text{ or }j\in A^{c}}}c_{A}-\sum_{A\atop{\blacklozenge\notin A\atop i,j\in A}}c_{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\atop{\blacklozenge\in A\atop i\in A^{c}}}c_{A}+\sum_{A\atop{\blacklozenge,i\in A\atop j\in A^{c}}}c_{A}-\sum_{A\atop{\blacklozenge\notin A\atop i,j\in A}}c_{A}.

By symmetry in
i,j
we may assume that
i\neq\blacklozenge
, and
i\neq j
holds anyway.
Then by the first assertion of Lemma
3.2
the last two summands above cancel.
Hence we have that

\alpha_{ij}=\sum_{A\atop{\blacklozenge\in A\atop i\in A^{c}}}c_{A}=\psi_{\blacklozenge}(\underline{c}),

using the definition of the dual
\psi
-classes (
8
) and Lemma
3.2
.
Furthermore, comparing with (
9
),
\circ_{i,j}
is indeed minus the
\mathsf{Com}
-action.

Finally, we consider the other composition (
24
), that can be handled similarly.
We obtain the linear combination of decorated trees

\displaystyle\sum_{A}c_{A}\left(T_{A^{c},A}\left(\mathbf{s}u\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{\blacklozenge}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-T_{A^{c},A}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}u\nabla(x_{\blacklozenge}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-d_{\mathsf{BV}^{\prime}_{\geq 3}}\left(\sum_{A}c_{A}\left(T_{A^{c},A}\left(\mathbf{s}x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i},\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{\blacklozenge}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\left.T_{A^{c},A}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}x_{\blacklozenge}\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=:-d_{\mathsf{BV}^{\prime}_{\geq 3}}X^{\prime}.

This is cohomologous to
d_{c}X^{\prime}
. Again the edge contraction on the edge
\blacklozenge
of the first summand does not yield a contribution due to the closedness
d_{c}\sum_{A}c_{A}T_{A}=0
. The other terms give:

\displaystyle d_{c}X^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{A}c_{A}\left(-T_{A}\left(\sum_{i<j\in A^{c}\sqcup\{*\}}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)-T_{A}\left(\sum_{i<j\in S\sqcup A^{c}\sqcup\{*\}\atop\text{$i$ or $j$}\in S}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\left.+T_{A\sqcup A^{c}}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}(\prod_{i\in A^{c}}x_{i})\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{A}c_{A}T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S\sqcup A^{c}}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)+\sum_{j<k}T_{A\sqcup A^{c}}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij}\sum_{A\atop j,k\in A}c_{A}\right).

We note that
\sum_{A\atop j,k\in A}c_{A}=\psi_{0}(\underline{c})
is independent of the choice of
j,k
, see Lemma
3.2
and the definition of
\psi_{0}
in (
8
).
Comparing to (
9
) we get the desired formula.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

The operation

\circ_{i,j}:\mathrm{gr}_{2n-2}H^{2n-2}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(1,n)\!))\otimes\mathrm{gr}_{2n^{\prime}-6}H^{2n^{\prime}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n^{\prime})\!))\\
\cong\mathsf{Com}(\!(n+2)\!)_{S_{2}}\otimes\mathsf{Com}(\!(n^{\prime})\!)\\
\to\mathrm{gr}_{2n+2n^{\prime}-6}H^{2n+2n^{\prime}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(1,n+n^{\prime}-2)\!))\cong\mathsf{Com}(\!(n+n^{\prime})\!)_{S_{2}}

agrees with minus the cyclic operadic composition in
\mathsf{Com}
, using the identification (
18
).

Proof.

The cohomology
\mathrm{gr}_{2n-2}H^{2n-2}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(1,n)\!))
is spanned by graphs
with one vertex decorated by the top-weight part of
\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}}
, which is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Com}
, and a tadpole.
Hence we have that

\mathrm{gr}_{2n-2}H^{2n-2}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(1,n)\!))\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2n-2}H^{2n-2}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n+2)\!))_{S_{2}}.

We can then just use the (genus 0) result of Proposition
4.6
to conclude that the composition is indeed minus the composition of
\mathsf{Com}
.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

The operation

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}:\mathrm{gr}_{2n-8}H^{2n-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n)\!))\cong\mathsf{HyCom}(\!(n)\!)^{-2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\to\mathrm{gr}_{2n-6}H^{2n-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n)\!))\cong\mathsf{Com}(\!(n)\!)\cong\mathbb{Q}

is dual to the map

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}\cong\mathsf{Com}(\!(n)\!)^{*}\cong H^{0}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n})\xrightarrow{\psi_{j}\wedge-}\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n})

of multiplication with the
\psi
-class at the
j
-th marking.

Proof.

As before, we begin with a representative

y:=\sum_{A}c_{A}T_{A}

of a cohomology class in
\mathrm{gr}_{2n-6}H^{2n-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2})(\!(0,n+1)\!)\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2n-6}H^{2n-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,n+1)\!))
.
In particular, the coefficients
c_{A}
satisfy (
7
).

First assume that
j=0
.
Then

\Delta_{0}y=-\sum_{A}c_{A}T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}u\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\notin A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)=d_{\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2}}\sum_{A}c_{A}T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}x_{*}\prod_{i\notin A}x_{i},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right).

This is cohomologous to

-d_{c}\sum_{A}c_{A}T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}x_{*}\prod_{i\notin A}x_{i},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)=\sum_{A}c_{A}T\left(\sum_{i<j\in A}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij}\right)=\sum_{i<j}\left(\sum_{A\atop i,j\in A}c_{A}\right)T(\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ij})

But the term in parentheses is independent of
i,j
by (
7
), and equal to
\psi_{0}(y)
.
Hence
\Delta_{0}y
is cohomologous to

\psi_{0}(x)T\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i<j\in S}x_{i})\right)

and the result is shown in this case.

Next suppose that
j>0
.
Then

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}y

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\atop j\in A}c_{A}T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\notin A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}u\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)+\sum_{A\atop j\notin A}c_{A}T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}u\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\notin A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=-d_{\widetilde{\mathsf{BV}}^{!}_{\geq 2}}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{A\atop j\in A}c_{A}T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}\nabla(x_{*}\prod_{i\notin A}x_{i}),\mathbf{s}\prod_{i\in A}x_{i}\right)+\sum_{A\atop j\notin A}c_{A}T_{A}\left(\mathbf{s}x_{*}\prod_{i\notin A}x_{i},\mathbf{s}\nabla(\prod_{i\in A}x_{i})\right)\right)}_{=:y^{\prime}}.

Hence
\Delta_{j}y
is cohomologous to
d_{c}y^{\prime}
. We compute further:

\displaystyle d_{c}y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=+\sum_{A\atop j\in A}c_{A}T\left(\sum_{i<k\atop i\text{ or }k\in A^{c}}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ik}\right)-\sum_{A\atop j\notin A}c_{A}T\left(\sum_{i<k\in A}\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ik}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<k}\alpha_{ik}T(\mathbf{s}\Omega_{ik})

with

\alpha_{ik}=\sum_{A\atop{j\in A\atop i\text{ or }k\in A^{c}}}c_{A}-\sum_{A\atop{j\in A^{c}\atop i,k\in A}}c_{A}.

Analogously to the derivation below (
25
)
we see that
\alpha_{ik}=\psi_{j}(y)
, independently of
i,k
, and we are done.

∎

Remark 4.10
.

We do not consider the explicit form of
\eta_{i,j}
here, because, as we shall see, the map will not be important for our cohomology computations.

5.
Proof of Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
and
1.4

5.1.
Combinatorial Description of
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
and
\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)

We shall describe the complex
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
combinatorially.
Elements can be seen as linear combinations of decorated connected graphs of loop order
g
with
r
legs.
The vertices
v
of valence
r_{v}\geq 3
are decorated by elements of
D\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(r_{v})\!)
. Vertices of valence two are implicitly decorated by the dual BV operator
\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}
.
No bivalent vertex can have a neighbour that is also a bivalent vertex.
Hence, instead of drawing bivalent vertices, we may equivalently consider
\geq
trivalent graphs some of whose edges and legs are marked.
A marked edge represents a pair of edge with a
\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}
-decorated bivalent vertex in the middle, as indicated in the following picture.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The only difference between
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
and
\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
is that the graphs generating
\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
cannot have marked legs, only marked internal edges.

The marked edges carry degree
+1
and weight
2
, which are the degree and weight of
\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}
.
The normal edges carry degree and weight 0.

We also note that the decorations by
D\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(r_{v})\!)
on vertices
v
can be understood as given by a linear combination of trees.
Hence, we may even consider
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
as generated by graphs with three different types of edges.
In this picture, the tree-edges carry degree
+1
and weight
0
.
The decorations by
\mathsf{BV}^{*}
on the vertices of the trees carry the cohomological degree of
\mathsf{BV}^{*}
, and contribute twice that degree to the weight.
But for simplicity, we shall restrict to the two types of edges above in this section, and keep the tree-edges implicit in the decoration of the vertices.

5.2.
Spectral sequence for
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})

The theorems are shown by studying a natural spectral sequence converging to
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})
, and similarly on
\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})
. We only state explicitly the former case, with the understanding that one can proceed analogously for
\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})
.

Let us define

\mathcal{F}_{p}\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)\subset\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)

to be the subspace spanned by graphs that have at most
p
bivalent vertices.
This subspace is closed under the differential, since the differential (—contracting edges—) cannot increase the number of bivalent vertices.
We may hence consider the spectral sequence
E^{\bullet}
associated to this filtration.
By finite dimensionality this spectral sequence converges to the cohomology

E^{\bullet}\Rightarrow H\left(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)\right).

We shall consider the various pages of this spectral sequence.
To this end, note that the differential on
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
has the form

d=d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}+d_{0}+d_{1}

where
d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}
is the differential induced from
D\mathsf{BV}^{*}
,
d_{0}
contracts an edge between two vertices of valence
\geq 3
and
d_{1}
contracts an edge between a bivalent and a higher valent vertex.

Similarly, we endow
\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
with the identically defined filtration, and by the same reasoning we obtain a spectral sequence

E_{A}^{\bullet}\Rightarrow H\left(\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)\right).

5.3.
E^{0}
-page and convergence

The associated graded complex (
E^{0}
-page) can be identified with
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
, but equipped with the differential
d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}+d_{0}
, that is, the part of the original differential that leaves invariant the number of bivalent vertices in graphs.
Recall from Section
5.1
that we may understand
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!)
as a vector space generated by decorated graphs with two types of edges – normal and marked.
But any graph with edges of two colors can be considered a nested graph, with the marked edges the edges of the outer graph, and the normal edges being part of graphs decorating every vertex of the outer graph.

Hence we also have the identification of modular sequences

\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})=\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})),

using the construction
\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}
of (
6
).
The differential on the
E^{0}
page
d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}+d_{0}
only acts on the inner object
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})
, and agrees with the differential there.
Hence by the Künneth formula we have

E^{1}:=H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,r)\!),d_{D\mathsf{BV}^{*}}+d_{0})\cong\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*}))).

Proposition 5.1
.

Suppose that there is a
p\geq 0
such that for all
g,n
and weights
W\geq 6g-6+2n-2p
the weight
W
cohomology of
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(g,n)\!)
is concentrated in degree
W
.
Then the weight
6g-6+2n-2p
-parts of the spectral sequences above converge to the cohomology on the
E^{2}
-page, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+2n-2p}H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))

\displaystyle\cong H(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+2n-2p}\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*}))),d_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+2n-2p}H(\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))

\displaystyle\cong H(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+2n-2p}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*}))),d_{1}).

By Corollary
4.3
we hence immediately obtain the following.

Corollary 5.2
.

The spectral sequences converges on the
E^{2}
page in top- and top
-2
-weight, that is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+4n}H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))\cong H(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+4n}\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*}))),d_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8+4n}H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))\cong H(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8+4n}\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*}))),d_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+4n}H(\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))\cong H(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+4n}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*}))),d_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8+4n}H(\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))\cong H(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8+4n}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*}))),d_{1}).

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

We only consider the case of the Feynman transform – for the amputated Feynman transform the argument is analogous.

Consider a graph
\Gamma\in\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})))(\!(g,n)\!)
.
Suppose it has
N
vertices, with each vertex
v
decorated by an element of some

\mathrm{gr}_{6g_{v}-6+2n_{v}-2p_{v}}H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g_{v},n_{v})\!))
.
Suppose that
\Gamma
has
e
internal edges and there are markings on
n^{\prime}
of the
n
external legs.
Then the genus of
\Gamma
is

g=e+1+\sum_{v}(g_{v}-1)

and the weight of
\Gamma
is

(26)

W_{\Gamma}:=2e+2n^{\prime}+\sum_{v}(6g_{v}-6+2n_{v}-2p_{v})=6g-6+4n-2\left(n-n^{\prime}+\sum_{v}p_{v}\right).

Here we also use that
\sum_{v}n_{v}=n+2e
, the number of half-edges.
That means that to make a weight
6g-6+4n-2p
graph, all the numbers
p_{v}
at the vertices must be
\leq p
.
Hence we can use the assumption that the cohomology of
H(\mathrm{Feyn}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*}))
in those weights is concentrated in degree equal to the weight.
Concretely, suppose that
\Gamma
has weight
W_{\Gamma}\geq 6g-6+4n-2p
.
Then it has degree

e+n^{\prime}+\sum_{v}(6g_{v}-6+2n_{v}-2p_{v})=W_{\Gamma}-e-n^{\prime}.

The differential on the
E^{q}
-page of the spectral sequence reduces the number (of bivalent vertices)
e+n^{\prime}
by exactly
q
.
But the same differential leaves invariant the weight, and increases the degree by +1. Hence by the above formulas for weight and degree this differential needs to vanish unless
q=1
.
∎

5.4.
Proof of parts (1) and (2) of Theorem
1.2

The proof of part (2) of Theorem
1.2
trivially follows from the fact that
\mathrm{gr}_{0}\mathsf{BV}^{*}=\mathsf{Com}^{*}
.

Part (1) for the non-amputated case
\mathrm{Feyn}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})
follows immediately from the formula (
26
) of the weight
W_{\Gamma}
of a graph
\Gamma\in\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})))(\!(g,n)\!)
, from which it is clear that
W_{\Gamma}\leq 6g-6+4n
.

The analogous formula in the amputated case reads

W_{\Gamma}=6g-6+2n-\sum_{v}p_{v},

since no leg can be marked. From this it is clear that the nontrivial weights in
H(\mathrm{AFeyn}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*}))
must be
\leq 6g-6+2n
.

5.5.
E^{1}
page in top-weight and proof of part (3) of Theorem
1.2

By Corollary
5.2
we have that the top-weight part
\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+4n}H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))
agrees with the cohomology of the
E^{1}
-page
\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+2n-2p}\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})))
with differential
d_{1}
.
By the proof of Proposition
5.1
above we know that graphs contributing to the top-weight piece of
\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)))
must have all external legs marked and each vertex
v
must be decorated by an element of the top-weight cohomology
\mathrm{gr}_{6g_{v}-6+2n_{v}}H^{k_{v}}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g_{v},n_{v})\!))
.
But by (
18
)
this cohomology vanishes unless each
g_{v}=0
and
k_{v}=2n_{v}-6
, and then it can be identified with
\mathsf{Com}(\!(n_{v})\!)\cong\mathbb{Q}
.

Hence, as a graded vector space we may identify

(27)

\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+4n}\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)))\cong\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!)[-6g+6-3n]\otimes\mathrm{sgn}_{n}

with the commutative graph complex, up to a degree shift and an extra sign factor if one also wants to preserve the
S_{n}
action.
To see that the degree shift is indeed by
6g-6+3n

consider a graph
\Gamma
of genus
g
with
n
external legs and
e
(non-leg-)edges.
The cohomological degree of this graph in
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!)
is
-e
.
The degree of the same graph
\Gamma
interpreted as an element of
\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))
is

\sum_{v\in V\Gamma}(2n_{v}-6)+e+n=4e+2n-6|V\Gamma|+e+n=6g-6+3n-e,

so that the degree shift is indeed
6g-6+3n
.

Next, we study the differential
d_{1}
on the
E_{1}
page.
The differential acts by removing one bivalent vertex, contracting an edge incident to that bivalent vertex.
There are three cases to consider:

(i)

The bivalent vertex sits between two different vertices.

\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}

(ii)

The bivalent vertex connects twice to the same vertex.

\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}

(iii)

The bivalent vertex is at an external leg.

\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}
j

We shall accordingly split our differential
d_{1}
into three terms

d_{1}=d_{1}^{(i)}+d_{1}^{(ii)}+d_{1}^{(iii)}

that we will discuss one-by-one.
First, in the top-weight case we have
d_{1}^{(ii)}=0
, since it produces a vertex of positive genus, and there are no such in top-weight.
Similarly,
d_{1}^{(iii)}
produces a vertex
v
with decoration of weight
6g_{v}-6+2n_{v}+2
, and again there are no possible decorations of that weight, the maximum being
6g_{v}-6+2n_{v}
.

Hence the only remaining piece is
d_{1}=d_{1}^{(i)}
.
Comparing the definitions, this piece agrees with the operation
\circ_{i,j}
of Section
4.2
applied to the two vertices
v,w
neighboring our bivalent vertex.
But by Proposition
4.6
this operation agrees with minus the operadic composition in
\mathsf{Com}
.
Hence the identification (
27
) is indeed an identification of dg vector spaces, if one considers the Feynman transform with minus the usual differential – but that we can do, see Remark
2.3
.
Putting all pieces together we have shown that

\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+4n}H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))\cong H(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!))[-6g+6-3n]\otimes\mathrm{sgn}_{n}

as desired.

The analogous amputated case is shown in the same manner. One just does not have the markings on external legs, and hence misses the corresponding degree shift of
n
and weight shift of
2n
, and the multiplication by the sign representation of
S_{n}
. This then finishes the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
∎

5.6.
Top
-2
weight and proof of Theorem
1.4

We may proceed similarly for the case of top
-2
-weight.
That is, consider a graph
\Gamma\in\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8+4n}\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)))
.
Again by the analysis of the proof of Proposition
5.1
there are three cases to be considered:

A.

The graph
\Gamma
has a single vertex
v
decorated by the top
-2
-weight cohomology

\mathrm{gr}_{6g_{v}-8+2n_{v}}H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g_{v},n_{v})\!)).

All other vertices
v^{\prime}
are decorated by the top-weight cohomology.
That is, arguing as in the previous subsection, all the other vertices are of genus zero and trivially decorated by
\mathbb{Q}
.
All legs of
\Gamma
are marked.
By (
19
) we also know that the special vertex must have genus either zero or one, and we may accordingly distinguish two sub-cases:

A0.

Here the special vertex
v
has genus zero, i.e., a decoration in

\mathrm{gr}_{2n_{v}-8}H^{2n_{v}-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(0,n_{v})\!))\cong\mathsf{HyCom}(\!(n_{v})\!)^{2}.

A1.

The special vertex
v
has genus one, i.e., a decoration in
\mathrm{gr}_{2n_{v}-2}H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(1,n_{v})\!))\cong\mathsf{Com}(\!(n_{v}+2)\!)_{S_{2}}
.

B.

All vertices of
\Gamma
have top weight, and are hence of genus zero and trivially (
\mathbb{Q}
-)decorated.
All legs of
\Gamma
except for one, say the
j
-th, are marked.

Accordingly, we split

\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8+4n}\mathbb{M}\mathrm{Free}_{1}(H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))):=V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)\oplus V_{A1}(\!(g,n)\!)\oplus V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!)

into graded subspaces, each spanned by graphs of the respective type.
As in the previous subsection we split the differential as
d_{1}=d_{1}^{(i)}+d_{1}^{(ii)}+d_{1}^{(iii)}
.
These pieces of the differential map between the subspaces as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{A1}(\!(g,n)\!)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{(ii)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{(iii)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{(i)}}

Again we may use Propositions
4.6
-
4.8
to obtain a very explicit combinatorial description of the different pieces of the differential:
In particular, elements of
V_{A1}(\!(g,n)\!)
can be seen as undecorated graphs (as in
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})
), but with one special vertex. All vertices are required to have valence
\geq 3
, except the special vertex which may also have valence
1
or
2
.
The differential
d_{1}^{(i)}
acts by contracting an edge between an arbitrary pair of distinct vertices.

Lemma 5.3
.

The complex
(V_{A1}(\!(g,n)\!),d_{1}^{(i)})
is acyclic except for the case of
(g,n)=(1,1)
, when it has one-dimensional cohomology spanned by the graph

(28)

*
1

Proof.

This statement has appeared in the literature on graph complexes in many closely analogous forms. We reproduce here the standard argument, originally due to Lambrechts-Volic
[
25
]
, for the reader’s convenience.

First, consider the decomposition

V_{A1}(\!(g,n)\!)=\hbox to100.72pt{\vbox to32.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 50.36108pt\lower-8.16864pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-50.36108pt}{-3.00891pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}{
{}{}{}}{{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}}{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}
}{{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}}{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}
}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}

with
V_{A1}^{\prime}\subset V_{A1}(\!(g,n)\!)
the subspace spanned by graphs for which the special vertex has valence
1
, and
V_{A1}^{\prime\prime}\subset V_{A1}
the subspace spanned by graphs for which the special vertex has valence
\geq 2
. The arrows indicate the different pieces of the differential
d_{1}^{(i)}
between the subspaces.
In particular the piece
f
of the differential contracts the unique edge at the special vertex

f:\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to42.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.42638pt\lower-25.8073pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.67 15.75 C 6.67 19.43 3.68 22.41 0 22.41 C -3.68 22.41 -6.67 19.43 -6.67 15.75 C -6.67 12.07 -3.68 9.08 0 9.08 C 3.68 9.08 6.67 12.07 6.67 15.75 Z M 0 15.75}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

The map
f
is surjective for all
(g,n)
, and injective for
(g,n)\neq(1,1)
. For
(g,n)=(1,1)
the kernel is spanned by the graph (
28
). From these observations and simple homological algebra considerations (see
[
28
, Lemma 2.1]
) the lemma follows.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

For
(g,n)\neq(1,1)
the projection

V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)\oplus V_{A1}(\!(g,n)\!)\oplus V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!)\to V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)\oplus V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!)

is a quasi-isomorphism of dg vector spaces and hence

\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8+4n}H(\mathrm{Feyn}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))\cong H(V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)\oplus V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!),d_{1}^{(i)}+d_{1}^{(iii)}).

We next consider the individual summands of the complex in the corollary.

Lemma 5.5
.

We have the isomorphisms of complexes

(29)

\displaystyle(V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!),d_{1}^{(i)})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!)[-6g+6-3n]

(30)

\displaystyle(V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!),d_{1}^{(i)})

\displaystyle\cong\oplus_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!)[-6g+7-3n].

Proof.

Case
V_{A0}
: The graded vector space
V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)
is generated by graphs with one special vertex
v
that is decorated by an element of
\mathrm{gr}_{2n_{v}-8}H^{2n_{v}-8}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(0,n_{v})\!))\cong\mathsf{HyCom}^{2}(\!(n_{v})\!)
, while all other (at least trivalent) vertices
w
are decorated by elements of
\mathrm{gr}_{2n_{w}-6}H^{2n_{v}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(0,n_{w})\!))\cong\mathbb{Q}
.
The edges and legs are all marked, and hence carry degree
+1
and weight
2
.

On the other hand,
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!)
is generated by graphs with one special vertex decorated by
\mathsf{HyCom}^{2}(\!(n_{v})\!)
, and all other vertices decorated by
\mathbb{Q}
.
The edges carry degree
+1
.

Hence we can identify
V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)
and
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!)
as graded vector spaces, up to a degree shift.
To compute the degree shift, consider a graph
\Gamma\in\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!)
with
e
(non-leg-)edges. Its degree in
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!)
is
-e-2
.
On the other hand, the same graph considered as a generator of

V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)
has degree

2n_{v}-8+\sum_{w\in V\Gamma\atop w\neq v}(2n_{w}-6)+e+n=6g-6+3n-e-2,

so that the degree shift is indeed
6g-6+3n
.

It remains to check that the differential on both complexes is the same. But this is a direct consequence of Proposition
4.7
, arguing analogously to the proof of part (3) of Theorem
1.2
in Section
5.5
.

Case
V_{B}
: Here we proceed similarly.
Generators of
V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!)
are graphs with all vertices
w
decorated by an element of
\mathrm{gr}_{2n_{v}-8}H^{2n_{v}-6}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(0,n_{w})\!))\cong\mathbb{Q}
.
All edges and legs are marked, except for one leg.
On the other hand, each summand of
\oplus_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!)
is generated by graphs all of whose vertices are decorated by
\mathsf{Com}(\!(n_{w})\!)\cong\mathbb{Q}
.
We think of the
j
-th summand of the direct sum as generated by graphs with the
j
-th leg distinguished.
Hence we have an identification of graded vector spaces

V_{B}\cong\oplus_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!)[-6g+7-3n],

with the
j
-th summand on the right-hand side corresponding to the sub-vector space of
V_{B}
spanned by graphs whose leg
j
is not marked. (Remember that the legs are numbered
1,\dots,n
.)
To check the degree shift above, we consider again a generating graph
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!)
with
e
edges. It has degree
-e
in
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!)
.
Considered as an element of
V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!)
it has degree

\sum_{w\in V\Gamma}(2n_{v}-6)+e+n-1=6g-6+3n-e-1,

so that the degree shift is
6g-7+3n
.
Again, we need to check that the differentials on both sides of (
30
) agree. But this follows from Proposition
4.8
, again as in Section
5.5
.
∎

By Lemma
2.1
the previous lemma implies that

\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{k}(\mathrm{Feyn}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(g,n)\!)))\cong\ker[d_{1}^{(iii)}]^{k}\oplus\coker[d_{1}^{(iii)}]^{k-1}

with

[d_{1}^{(iii)}]^{k}:H^{k}(V_{A0})\to H^{k+1}(V_{B})

the cohomology map induced by
d_{1}^{(iii)}
.
We investigate further the map
[d_{1}^{(iii)}]^{k}
.
To this end let
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!)^{k}
be a generator and fix some
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Then we define

\psi_{j}^{*}\Gamma\in\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!)^{k+2}

as follows.

•

If the vertex
v
of
\Gamma
adjacent to leg
j
is not the special vertex, we set
\psi_{j}^{*}\Gamma:=0
.

•

Suppose that the vertex
v
of
\Gamma
adjacent to leg
j
is the special vertex, and decorated by
x\in\mathsf{HyCom}(\!(n_{v})\!)^{-2}
. The we define
\psi_{j}^{*}\Gamma
as the same graph
\Gamma
, except that the decoration
x
at
v
is replaced by
\psi_{j}(x)
, see (
8
) above. This is the dual operation of multiplying with the
\psi
-class at marking
j
, hence we use the notation “
\psi_{j}^{*}\Gamma
”.

Finally, we assemble the maps
\psi_{j}^{*}
for
j=1,\dots,n
into one morphism

\displaystyle\Psi^{k}_{\wedge}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{k}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!))\to\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}H^{k+2}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!))

\displaystyle\Psi_{\wedge}^{k}(\Gamma)=(\psi_{1}^{*}\Gamma,\dots,\psi_{n}^{*}\Gamma).

We then have:

Lemma 5.6
.

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{6g-6+3n-k}(V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{6g-5+3n-k}(V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{-k}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}H^{-k+2}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})(\!(g,n)\!)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[d_{1}^{(iii)}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\wedge}^{-k}}

Proof.

This follows directly from Proposition
4.9
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

We just have to assemble the results above.
By Corollary
5.2
we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8+4n}H^{6g-6+3n-k}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))

\displaystyle\cong H^{6g-6+3n-k}\left(V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)\oplus V_{A1}(\!(g,n)\!)\oplus V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!),d_{1}^{(i)}+d_{1}^{(ii)}+d_{1}^{(iii)}\right).

By Corollary
5.4
this is isomorphic to

H^{6g-6+3n-k}\left(V_{A0}(\!(g,n)\!)\oplus V_{B}(\!(g,n)\!),d_{1}^{(i)}+d_{1}^{(iii)}\right).

By Lemma
2.1
this is isomorphic to

\ker[d_{1}^{(iii)}]^{6g-6+3n-k}\oplus\coker[d_{1}^{(iii)}]^{6g-7+3n-k}.

By Lemma
5.6
this is in turn isomorphic to

\ker\Psi_{\wedge}^{-k}\oplus\coker\Psi_{\wedge}^{-k-1}.\qed

5.7.
Proof of Theorem
1.3

The amputated analogue of the previous subsection is again easier: Since there are no markings on the external legs, one just omits the summand
V_{B}
from the above discussion, as well as the map
d_{1}^{(iii)}
.
The analogous versions of Lemma
5.5
and Corollary
5.4
then state that for
(g,n)\neq(1,1)
we have that

\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{6g-6+2n-k}(\mathrm{AFeyn}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{-k}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!)).

Just mind that in comparison to (
29
) we here have a degree shift of
n
less, and a weight shift of
2n
less, due to the legs not being marked in the amputated case.
∎

5.8.
Proof of Corollary
1.8

Corollary
1.8
follows immediately by combining Theorem
1.1
with Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
.

6.
Resolutions of
\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}
in weight
\leq 2

To show Theorem
1.5
we will need to use a resolution of the weight
\leq 2
-part of
\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}
that is more suitable for combinatorial arguments.
We also provide a similar presentation of
\mathsf{BV}^{*}
.

6.1.
\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}

Recall that the weight grading on
\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}
is the same as grading by the cohomological degree.
We consider the weight-truncated cyclic cooperad

\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}_{\leq 2}:=\bigoplus_{W\leq 2}\mathrm{gr}_{W}\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}=\mathrm{gr}_{0}\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}\oplus\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}\subset\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}

consisting of elements of weight
\leq 2
.

We need a combinatorial model
\mathsf{HyC}^{*}
for
\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}_{\leq 2}
from
[
28
]
that we briefly recall.

•

The weight 0 part is just the commutative cyclic cooperad

\mathrm{gr}_{0}\mathsf{HyC}^{*}=\mathrm{gr}_{0}\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}=\mathsf{Com}^{*}.

•

A basis of the weight 2 part
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{HyC}^{*}(\!(r)\!)
is given by elements

E_{ij}=E_{ji}
(
1\leq i\neq j\leq r
) of degree
1
, elements
\psi_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq r
) of degree 2 and elements
\delta_{A}=\delta_{A^{c}}
of degree 2 with
A
ranging over subsets of
\{1,\dots,r\}
such that
|A|,|A^{c}|\geq 2
.

The differential is such that

dE_{ij}=\psi_{i}+\psi_{j}-\sum_{A\subset\{1,\dots,r\}\atop i\in A,j\in A^{c}}\delta_{A}.

Describing the cyclic cooperad structure on
\mathsf{HyC}^{*}
amounts to describing the
\mathsf{Com}^{c}
-coaction on the weight 2 part.
For
S
a finite set and
B\subset S
a subset we seek to define the coaction

\Delta_{B}:\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{HyC}^{*}(\!(S)\!)\to\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{HyC}^{*}(\!(B\sqcup\{*\}))\otimes\mathsf{Com}^{*}(\!(B^{c}\sqcup\{*^{\prime}\}))\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{HyC}^{*}(\!(B\sqcup\{*\})).

This will be defined as follows on the basis elements:

(31)

\displaystyle\Delta_{B}E_{ij}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}E_{ij}&\text{if $i,j\in B$}\\
E_{i*}&\text{if $i\in B$, $j\in B^{c}$}\\
E_{j*}&\text{if $j\in B$, $i\in B^{c}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(32)

\displaystyle\Delta_{B}\psi_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\psi_{i}&\text{if $i\in B$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(33)

\displaystyle\Delta_{B}\delta_{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\delta_{A}&\text{if $A\subsetneq B$}\\
\delta_{A^{c}}&\text{if $B\subsetneq A$}\\
\psi_{*}&\text{if $B=A$ or $B=A^{c}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

6.2.
\mathsf{BV}^{*}

Recall that on
\mathsf{BV}^{*}
we define the weight grading as twice the grading by cohomological degree.
We consider the weight-truncated version

\mathsf{BV}^{*}_{\leq 2}:=\bigoplus_{W\leq 2}\mathrm{gr}_{W}\mathsf{BV}^{*}=\mathrm{gr}_{0}\mathsf{BV}^{*}\oplus\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}\subset\mathsf{BV}^{*}.

Concretely, we have that
\mathrm{gr}_{0}\mathsf{BV}^{*}=\mathsf{Com}^{*}
.
We want to describe explicitly the part
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}
.
Dualizing the discussion of Section
3.3
we see that an explicit basis of
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}(r)
is given by the elements
\omega_{ij}=\omega_{ji}
for
1\leq i,j\leq r
.
In this basis the cyclic structure takes the following form.
A permutation
\sigma\in S_{r}
acts on the basis element
\omega_{ij}
(from the left) as

\sigma\omega_{ij}=\omega_{\sigma(i)\sigma(j)}.

The action of
S_{r}
on
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}(r)
extends to an action of
S_{r+1}
. Let
\tau=(01)\in S_{r+1}
be the transposition of symbols
0
and
1
. Then we have that for
i\leq j

\tau\omega_{ij}=\begin{cases}\omega_{ij}-\omega_{1j}-\omega_{1i}+\omega_{11}&\text{for $i\geq 2$}\\
-\omega_{1j}+\omega_{11}&\text{for $i=1$, $j\geq 2$}\\
\omega_{11}&\text{for $i=j=1$}\end{cases}.

Unfortunately, the above basis is not very canonical if one considers
\mathsf{BV}^{*}
as a cyclic operad.
Hence we define here the alternative basis elements
of
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}(r)\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(r+1)\!)
:

\displaystyle E_{ij}

\displaystyle:=2\omega_{ij}-\omega_{ii}-\omega_{jj}=E_{ji}

\displaystyle E_{0i}

\displaystyle:=-\omega_{ii},

where we use indices
0,\dots,r
to label the inputs in the cyclic setting.

Lemma 6.1
.

The above elements
E_{ij}
with
0\leq i<j\leq r
form a basis of

\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(r+1)\!)\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}(r).

The
S_{r+1}\cong\mathrm{Bij}(\{0,\dots,r\})
-action on
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(r+1)\!)
operates on the basis elements as

(34)

\sigma\cdot E_{ij}=E_{\sigma(i)\sigma(j)},

where we identify
E_{ij}=E_{ji}
, and where
\sigma\in S_{r+1}
.
The
\mathsf{Com}^{*}
-comodule structure on the basis elements obeys the formula (
31
) for all
B\subset S
, in particular also
B
of cardinality
|B|=2
.

Proof.

It is clear that the elements
E_{ij}
form a basis.

We next check the formula (
34
) for the
S_{r+1}
-action.
If
\sigma\in S_{r}\subset S_{r+1}
, only permuting numbers
\geq 1
, then the formula is obvious.
Hence we just need to check that the morphism is compatible with the permutation
\tau=(01)
.
First suppose that
i,j\geq 2
.
Then we compute

\displaystyle\tau E_{ij}

\displaystyle=\tau(2\omega_{ij}-\omega_{ii}-\omega_{jj})

\displaystyle=2\omega_{ij}-2\omega_{i1}-2\omega_{1j}+2\omega_{11}-\omega_{ii}+2\omega_{i1}-\omega_{11}-\omega_{jj}+2\omega_{j1}-\omega_{11}

\displaystyle=2\omega_{ij}-\omega_{ii}-\omega_{jj}=E_{ij}=E_{\tau(i)\tau(j)}

\displaystyle\tau E_{0i}

\displaystyle=-\tau\omega_{ii}=-\omega_{ii}+2\omega_{1i}-\omega_{11}

\displaystyle=E_{1i}=E_{\tau(0)\tau(i)}

as desired.
Similarly, for
j\geq 2
we obtain:

\displaystyle\tau E_{1j}

\displaystyle=\tau(2\omega_{1j}-\omega_{11}-\omega_{jj})

\displaystyle=-2\omega_{1j}+2\omega_{11}-\omega_{11}-\omega_{jj}+2\omega_{j1}-\omega_{11}

\displaystyle=-\omega_{jj}=E_{0j}=E_{\tau(1)\tau(j)}

\displaystyle\tau E_{01}

\displaystyle=-\tau\omega_{11}=-\omega_{11}=E_{01}=E_{\tau(0)\tau(1)}

Hence (
34
) is established.

Finally, we have to check formula (
31
) describing the
\mathsf{Com}^{*}
-coaction

\Delta_{B}:\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(S)\!)\to\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(B\sqcup\{*\})).

We take the indexing sets to be
S=\{0,\dots,r\}
and

B=\{0,\dots,s\}\subset S
with
1\leq s\leq r-2
, without loss of generality.
Then we distinguish several cases:

•

For
1\leq i,j\leq s
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{B}E_{ij}=\Delta_{B}(2\omega_{ij}-\omega_{ii}-\omega_{jj})

\displaystyle=2\omega_{ij}-\omega_{ii}-\omega_{jj}=E_{ij}

•

For
1\leq i\in S
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{B}E_{0i}=-\Delta_{B}(\omega_{ii})

\displaystyle=-\omega_{ii}=E_{0i}

•

For
1\leq i\in B
,
j\in B^{c}
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{B}E_{ij}=\Delta_{B}(2\omega_{ij}-\omega_{ii}-\omega_{jj})

\displaystyle=2\omega_{i*}-\omega_{ii}-\omega_{**}=E_{i*}

•

For
j\in B^{c}
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{B}E_{0j}=-\Delta_{B}(\omega_{jj})

\displaystyle=-\omega_{**}=E_{0*}.

•

For
i,j\in B^{c}
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{B}E_{ij}=\Delta_{B}(2\omega_{ij}-\omega_{ii}-\omega_{jj})

\displaystyle=2\omega_{**}-\omega_{**}-\omega_{**}=0.

This shows the lemma.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Note that one might think that the lemma says that there is a map of cyclic cooperads

\mathsf{HyC}^{*}\to\mathsf{BV}^{*}_{\leq 2}

obtained by setting to zero the generators
\psi_{i}
and
\delta_{A}
of
\mathsf{HyC}^{*}
. This is however not true, because the cocomposition of
E_{ij}
in
\mathsf{HyC}^{*}
does not produce arity two elements, while the cocomposition in
\mathsf{BV}^{*}_{\leq 2}
does.
This defect can be repaired by extending the above morphism to an
\infty
-morphism, adding one higher homotopy. We shall not do this here since we want to avoid
\infty
-cooperadic constructions. However, the interested reader can read off the
\infty
-morphism from the map
\Phi
between the corresponding Feynman transforms introduced in Section
7.2
.

7.
\mathsf{HyCom}
and
\mathsf{BV}
graph complexes and proof of Theorem
1.5

7.1.
Combinatorial description of graph complexes

We begin by describing the weight 2 part of the graph complex
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom}^{*})
.
Since the above variant
\mathsf{HyC}^{*}
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}
in weights
\leq 2
we have a quasi-isomorphism

\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})\to\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}).

The graph complex
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})
consists of linear combinations of graphs with one special vertex decorated by
\mathsf{HyC}^{*}
, while all other vertices are undecorated.
We indicate the special vertex by a double circle.
The special vertex can carry three different decorations.
First, it can be decorated by a
\psi
-class, that we shall depict by an arrow

\scriptstyle\psi_{i}

Note that the special vertex must have valence at least three.

Finally, the special vertex may be decorated by
\delta_{A}
.
Pictorially, we replace the special vertex with two vertices connected by a marked edge.

Here the subset
A
of half-edges is connected to one vertex, and the complement to the other.

The differential consists of two parts,

d=d_{s}+d_{\mathsf{HyC}^{*}},

where
d_{s}
acts by splitting vertices, using the cooperadic cocomposition, and
d_{\mathsf{HyC}^{*}}
is induced from the differential on
\mathsf{HyC}^{*}
.
Concretely, since the differential on
\mathsf{HyC}^{*}
is only nontrivial on the
E_{ij}
-generator, we have the pictorial description

Finally, we turn to

\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*}).

The combinatorial description of this graph complex is similar to that of
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})

above. The collection of dg vector spaces
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})
consists of linear combinations of graphs with one special vertex.
This special is decorated by
E_{ij}
, using the basis
E_{ij}
of
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}
introduced in Section
6.2
.

We use the graphical encoding (
35
) to indicate such a decoration on the special vertex.
One important fact to note is, however, that in
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})
the special vertex may have valence 2, while in
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})
it needs to have valence
\geq 3
.
In particular, in the analog version of (
40
) for
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})
there are also terms for which the special vertex is bivalent.

7.2.
A morphism

We next describe a morphism of dg modular sequences

\Phi:\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})\to\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*}).

This morphism is defined as follows:

•

If
\Gamma\in\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
is a graph whose special vertex is decorated by
\delta_{A}
, then we set
\Phi(\Gamma)=0
.

•

If
\Gamma\in\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
is a graph whose special vertex is decorated by
E_{ij}
, then we set
\Phi(\Gamma)=\Gamma
, where we identify the basis element
E_{ij}
of
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{HyC}^{*}
with the corresponding basis element
E_{ij}
of
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathsf{BV}^{*}
, see
6.2
.

•

Finally, let
\Gamma\in\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
be a graph whose special vertex is decorated by
\psi_{i}
, with
i
corresponding to some half-edge at the special vertex.
Then we set
\Phi(\Gamma)=\Gamma^{\prime}
, with
\Gamma^{\prime}
obtained from
\Gamma
by the replacement

(41)

That is, we replace the special vertex in
\Gamma
by an ordinary vertex and add a new bivalent special vertex to the graph on the edge of the half-edge
i
. As usual, we put the new edge first in the ordering.

Lemma 7.1
.

The map
\Phi:\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})\to\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})

defined above is a morphism of dg modular sequences.

Proof.

It is clear that
\Phi
intertwines the symmetric group actions.
We shall check that it also intertwines the differentials.
Since the differential on
\mathsf{BV}^{*}
is zero we have to check that for any graph
\Gamma\in\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)

\Phi(d_{s}\Gamma+d_{\mathsf{HyC}^{*}}\Gamma)=d_{s}\Phi(\Gamma).

The splitting differential
d_{s}
can act either by splitting the special vertex, or a non-special vertex.
Since
\Phi(\Gamma)
is the same graph as
\Gamma
away from the special vertex, it is sufficient to consider the pieces of the differential that acts on the special vertex.
We naturally have to consider three different cases, according to the decoration of the special vertex of
\Gamma
.

•

Suppose the special vertex of
\Gamma
is decorated by
\delta_{A}
. Then
\Phi(\Gamma)=0
and
d_{\mathsf{HyC}^{*}}\Gamma=0
, so we have to check that
\Phi(d_{s}\Gamma)=0
.
Among the terms
d_{s}\Gamma
there are those with with their special vertex decorated by some
\delta_{A^{\prime}}
, and those for which the special vertex is decorated by some
\psi_{i}
, see (
39
).
The former terms are trivially sent to zero, so we only need to consider the latter. Pictorially,

d_{s}\Gamma=\hbox to57.31pt{\vbox to28.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.42638pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.96 0 C 1.96 1.08 1.08 1.96 0 1.96 C -1.08 1.96 -1.96 1.08 -1.96 0 C -1.96 -1.08 -1.08 -1.96 0 -1.96 C 1.08 -1.96 1.96 -1.08 1.96 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.96 0 C 1.96 1.08 1.08 1.96 0 1.96 C -1.08 1.96 -1.96 1.08 -1.96 0 C -1.96 -1.08 -1.08 -1.96 0 -1.96 C 1.08 -1.96 1.96 -1.08 1.96 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=0,

with the “1” over the edge indicating that this edge comes first in the order of edges.
Both terms cancel due to sign, given that the order of the two edges adjacent to the bivalent vertex is opposite.

•

Suppose that the special vertex of
\Gamma
is decorated by
E_{ij}
. Then
\Phi(\Gamma)=\Gamma
and the difference

d_{s}\Phi(\Gamma)-\Phi(d_{s}\Gamma)

consists only of those terms for which the special vertex is bivalent, namely:

(See also Remark
6.2
.)
But these terms are equal to
\Phi(d_{\mathsf{HyC}^{*}}\Gamma)
, see (
37
) for a picture of
d_{\mathsf{HyC}^{*}}\Gamma
and then (
41
) for the action of
\Phi
.

•

Finally, suppose the special vertex is decorated by
\psi_{i}
.
Then
d_{\mathsf{HyC}^{*}}\Gamma=0
and
\Phi(\Gamma)
is obtained from
\Gamma
by making the special vertex non-special, but adding a bivalent vertex.
Comparing the formulas for splitting the
\psi_{i}
-decorated vertex, the terms in
\Phi(d_{s}\Gamma)
match those in
d_{s}\Phi(\Gamma)
from splitting the new ordinary vertex. ∎

7.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.5

Given the map
\Phi
of the previous subsection, Theorem
1.5
follows from the following result.

Proposition 7.2
.

The map
\Phi:\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)\to\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
is a quasi-isomorphism for all
(g,n)\neq(1,0),(0,2)
.

Proof.

Let
(g,n)\neq(1,0),(0,2)
.
It is easy to see that
\Phi
is a surjective map.
Hence we have to check that the kernel of
\Phi
is acyclic,
H(\ker\Phi)=0
.
We first describe the kernel explicitly.
A basis of the kernel is given by (i) graphs
\Gamma
whose special vertex is decorated by
\delta_{A}
and (ii) linear combinations of two graphs, with
\psi
-decorations along one edge in a symmetric combination:

The basis elements (ii) may be considered as given by graphs with a marked edge instead of a vertex, the edge corresponding to the symmetric combination of
\psi
-decorations as above.

Next, we want to check that
H(\ker\Phi)=0
.
To this end, let
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyC}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
be a graph with
k
vertices. We say that the number of effective vertices of
\Gamma
is
k+1
if the special vertex is decorated by
\delta_{A}
and
k
otherwise.
We filter
\ker\Phi
by the number of effective vertices and consider the associated spectral sequence.
On the first page we see only the last two terms of the differential (
39
) replacing the
\delta_{A}
-decorated special vertex by a pair of vertices with a symmetric
\psi
-decoration.

\lxSVG@closescope }

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.65 0 L 19.69 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -40.95 1.58 L -59.06 19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

This map clearly is a bijection between the basis elements (i) and (ii) in the kernel. Hence our spectral sequence abuts to 0 and we have shown that
H(\ker\Phi)=0
as desired.
∎

7.4.
Blown-up picture and proof of Proposition
1.6

There is a different combinatorial way in which we may depict generators of
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
, which we call the “blown-up picture”, following
[
30
]
.
Concretely, we may remove the special vertex from the graph, and make the incident half-edges into external legs. The decoration
E_{ij}
is remembered by marking those legs corresponding to
i
and
j
with a symbol
\omega
, and the other legs by
\epsilon
.
For example:

The same graph complexes have appeared in the algebraic topology literature, and compute direct summands of the cohomology of embedding spaces of copies of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and one sphere into
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, see
[
14
]
.

In the special case
n=0
it is known that the above complexes can be simplified further, see
[
31
]
.
To this end, let

Y_{g}\subset\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!)

be the subcomplex spanned by graphs that have
(i) no
\epsilon
-decorated legs in the blown-up picture and (ii) no blown-up connected component that is a tadpole graph

D=\hbox to22.8pt{\vbox to33.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.40105pt\lower-14.02168pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

In the standard picture (i) means that the special vertex has valence 2.
Then we have:

Proposition 7.3
.

For
g\geq 2
the inclusion
Y_{g}\to\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!)
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

We work in the blown-up picture, and compute the cohomology of
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!)
.
We filter
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!)
by the number of (blown-up) connected components and consider the associated spectral sequence.
The differential on the first page leaves the number of blown-up components the same, so that the resulting complex is a sum of products of a connected graph complex.
The cohomology of the connected complex has been computed in
[
31
, Theorem 3.1, subcase “
m,n
even”]
, and shown to be equal to that of the subcomplex without
\epsilon
-legs, minus the tadpole graph
D
.
Hence, the cohomology of the
E^{1}
-page is the same as the cohomology of
Y_{g}
.
This means that the
E^{2}
-pages of the spectral sequences associated to
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!)
and of its subcomplex
Y_{g}
agree. All higher differentials in the spectral sequence vanish since there is no piece of the differential that can reduce the number of blown-up components of a graph without
\epsilon
-legs, nor is there a piece of the differential creating an
\epsilon
leg.
Hence our spectral sequence abuts at the
E^{2}
-page.
Furthermore, since
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!)
is finite dimensional, the spectral sequences converge to the cohomology.
Hence we conclude that the inclusion
Y_{g}\to\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!)
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.6
.

By Proposition
7.3
we may consider the quasi-isomorphic subcomplex
Y_{g}
.
Note that every graph generating
Y_{g}

has either one blown-up component with two legs, or two blown-up components with one leg each.

*
\omega
\omega

or

*
*
\omega
\omega

Accordingly, the complex
Y_{g}
splits into a direct sum of subcomplexes

\displaystyle Y_{g}

\displaystyle=Y_{g}^{1}\oplus Y_{g}^{2}.

Furthermore,
Y_{g}^{2}
is isomorphic to the genus
g
piece of the symmetric product

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(\oplus_{h\geq 2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(h,1)\!)^{no-tp}[-1])[-1],

where we restrict the sum to
h\geq 2
to account for the component
D
not being present in generators of
Y_{g}^{2}
.

Similarly,
Y_{g}^{2}
is isomorphic to the part of
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(g,2)\!)[-1]
that is antisymmetric under the exchange of the two hairs. Hence Proposition
1.6
follows.
∎

7.5.
Proofs of Corollaries
1.9
and
1.10

Corollary
1.9
follows by restricting the third statement of Corollary
1.8
to
n=0
and applying Theorem
1.5
and Proposition
1.6
.

Next consider Corollary
1.10
.
This is deduced from statement (2) of Corollary
1.8
and from Corollary
1.9
, using nontrivial classes in the commutative graph cohomology

H(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(g,1)\!))
.
The argument is identical to that leading to
[
28
, Corollary 1.3]
, and we recall here the main steps.
Firstly, as in loc. cit. we know that the dimensions of

\displaystyle H^{2g}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!))\text{ and }H^{2g+3}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(g,1)\!))

are at least exponentially growing as
g\to\infty
.
Hence so is the dimension of
H^{4g-k}(\mathrm{HMod}_{g,0}^{0})
for
k=6,9
by statement (2) of Corollary
1.8
.

Secondly, we also know that the dimensions of

(42)

H^{2g}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(g,1)\!))\text{ and }H^{2g+3}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(g,1)\!))

are at least exponentially growing as
g\to\infty
and that there is a nontrivial cohomology class in

(43)

H^{27}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(10,1)\!)).

As in Proposition
1.6
, let
\mathbb{W}_{g}^{k}
be the part of total genus
g
and degree
k
of the symmetric product

\mathbb{W}=\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\left(\bigoplus_{g\geq 2}H^{\bullet}\left(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(g,1)\!)\right)[-1]\right).

Then by Corollary
1.9
,
(\mathbb{W}_{g}^{k-1})^{*}
is a summand in
\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8}H^{6g-6-k}(\mathrm{HMod}_{g,0}^{0})
.
Now combining the non-trivial classes from (
42
) and (
43
) as factors in
\mathbb{W}
yields at least exponential growth of
H^{4g-k}(\mathrm{HMod}_{g,0}^{0})
for
k=9,12,15,16,19
.

5

5

Let
\operatorname{F}=\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com}^{*})
.
These numbers are obtained by considering the following summands of
\mathbb{W}_{g}^{k-1}
:

\displaystyle H^{2g^{\prime}}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(g^{\prime},1)\!)\right)[-1]\oplus H^{2g^{\prime}}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(g^{\prime},1)\!)\right)[-1]\subset\mathbb{W}_{2g^{\prime}}^{4g^{\prime}+2},\quad H^{2g^{\prime}}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(g^{\prime},1)\!)\right)[-1]\oplus H^{2g^{\prime}+3}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(g^{\prime},1)\!)\right)[-1]\subset\mathbb{W}_{2g^{\prime}}^{4g^{\prime}+5},

\displaystyle H^{2g^{\prime}+3}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(g^{\prime},1)\!)\right)[-1]\oplus H^{2g^{\prime}+3}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(g^{\prime},1)\!)\right)[-1]\subset\mathbb{W}_{2g^{\prime}}^{4g^{\prime}+8},\quad H^{2g^{\prime}}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(g^{\prime},1)\!)\right)[-1]\oplus H^{27}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(10,1)\!)\right)[-1]\subset\mathbb{W}_{g^{\prime}+10}^{2g^{\prime}+29},

\displaystyle H^{2g^{\prime}+3}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(g^{\prime},1)\!)\right)[-1]\oplus H^{27}\left(\operatorname{F}(\!(10,1)\!)\right)[-1]\subset\mathbb{W}_{g^{\prime}+10}^{2g^{\prime}+32}.

8.
Comparison of
H^{\bullet}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})
and
\mathsf{HyCom}
graph complexes and proof of Theorem
1.7

8.1.
Graph complex computing
\mathrm{gr}_{2}H_{c}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n})
– recollection from
[
28
]

Sam Payne and the last-named author
[
28
]
have identified a graph complex
X_{g,n}
that computes the weight 2 part of the compactly supported cohomology of the moduli spaces of curves
\mathrm{gr}_{2}H_{c}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n})
.
The complex has a very efficient description in terms of the Feynman transform of
\mathsf{BV}^{*}
as follows.

Recall that a tadpole in a graph is an edge connecting a vertex to itself.
Graphs contributing to the Feynman transform may have tadpoles.
Specifically, a graph
\Gamma\in\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
may have tadpoles of 4 different types:

•

Tadpoles at internal vertices.

Type 0:

•

Tadpoles at the special vertex, which can have either of three different types:

Type 1:

Type 2:

Type 3:

In other words, if the decoration at the special vertex is
E_{ij}
then tadpoles of type 1 (resp. type 2, type 3) are such that none (resp. one, two) of the half-edges
i
and
j
are part of the tadpole edge.

Let
I_{g,n}\subset\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
be the subspace spanned by graphs that have at least one tadpole of type 0, 2 or 3.

Lemma 8.1
.

The subspace
I_{g,n}\subset\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
is closed under the differential, that is,

dI_{g,n}\subset I_{g,n}.

Proof.

One checks that the differential cannot remove any of the tadpoles of type 0,2,3. At worst, it can transform a tadpole of type 2 into one of type 0.
∎

We then define, for
g,n
such that
2g+n\geq 3
, the quotient complex

X_{g,n}:=\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)/I_{g,n}.

We recall the main result from
[
28
]
.

Theorem 8.2
.

For all
(g,n)\neq(1,1)
such that
2g+n\geq 3
we have that

\mathrm{gr}_{2}H_{c}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n})\cong X_{g,n}.

8.2.
Proof of Proposition
1.7

To show Proposition
1.7
we first introduce an auxiliary graph complex
Z_{g,n}
using the tadpole terminology of the previous section.
Concretely, let

Z_{g,n}\subset\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)

be the graded subspace spanned by graphs that do not have any tadpole, of any of the types 0-3 above.
The differential (vertex splitting) cannot create a tadpole if there was none before, so that
Z_{g,n}
is a dg subspace.

Lemma 8.3
.

The inclusion
Z_{g,n}\to\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)
is a quasi-isomorphism for all
(g,n)
except
(g,n)=(1,0),(1,1)
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
[
2
, Lemma 5]
.
We have to check that the quotient

Q_{g,n}:=\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)/Z_{g,n}

is acyclic.
Note that a basis of
Q_{g,n}
is given by graphs with at least one tadpole, of any type.
For such a graph
\Gamma
let us say that a vertex is a thin tadpole vertex if it is non-special, carries a tadpole, and has valence
3
.

Let
k(\Gamma)
be the number of other vertices in
\Gamma
.
We filter
Q_{g,n}
by the numbers
k(\Gamma)
and consider the associated spectral sequence.
The first page is identified with
(Q_{g,n},d_{s}^{\prime})
, where
d_{s}^{\prime}
are those terms of the vertex splitting differential that create a thin tadpole vertex.
Concretely,
d_{s}^{\prime}
acts non-trivially only on vertices carrying a tadpole:

\displaystyle\mapsto 0

There is an obvious homotopy for this operation (by contracting the unique non-tadpole edge at a thin tadpole vertex). Hence one can show that
E_{1}:=H(Q_{g,n},d_{s}^{\prime})
is spanned by graphs that do not have tadpoles of types 0,1,2, using the tadpole terminology of Section
8.1
.
In other words,
E_{1}
is spanned by graphs that have a type 3 tadpole, but no other tadpole.
The differential on this page of the spectral sequence is given by splitting vertices.

We decompose
E_{1}
further into a direct sum

(g,n)\neq(1,0)
.

This morphism is clearly a bijection on basis elements, with the inverse given by contracting the unique non-tadpole edge at the special vertex. Here we are using that
(g,n)\neq(1,1)
, which implies that there must be a vertex at the other end of that edge.
But if
f
is a bijection, then by
[
28
, Lemma 2.1]
the complex
E_{1}
is acyclic, and we conclude that
H(Q_{g,n})=0
as desired.
∎

Note that we have morphisms of dg vector spaces

Z_{g,n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)\to X_{g,n}.

Next, we shall split
X_{g,n}
into two graded subspaces,

(44)

with
X_{g,n}^{\prime}
spanned by graphs that have a no tadpole, and
X_{g,n}^{\prime\prime}
spanned by graphs that have a tadpole.
The arrows indicate the pieces
d_{0},d_{1}
of the differential.
Note that

(45)

X_{g,n}^{\prime}\cong Z_{g,n}

is naturally identified with the image of
Z_{g,n}
in
X_{g,n}
. Furthermore, the graphs generating
X_{g,n}^{\prime\prime}
have exactly one tadpole, and this tadpole is of type 1.
Hence there is an isomorphism of graded vector spaces

(46)

T:Z_{g-1,n}\cong X_{g-1,n}^{\prime}\to X_{g,n}^{\prime\prime}

defined on a graph by adding one tadpole of type 1.

We next consider the decomposition (
44
) as a two-step filtration on
X_{g,n}
and study the associated spectral sequence.
The
E^{1}
-page of this spectral sequence has the form

\hbox to170.33pt{\vbox to38.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 85.16167pt\lower-8.84918pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
