Since we are considering a two-step filtration, all differentials on higher pages must vanish, and the spectral sequence converges to cohomology on the
E^{2}
page.
That is, we have that

H(X_{g,n})\cong\coker d_{1}\oplus\ker d_{1}.

To arrive at the first statement of Proposition
1.7
we just use the identifications (
45
) and (
46
) and Lemma
8.3
.
These show that the
E^{1}
-page of our spectral sequence is isomorphic to the cone of the morphism

\nabla_{g,n}:H(\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g-1,n)\!))\to H(\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))[2]

obtained by concatenating
T
and
d_{1}
.

The final statement of Proposition
1.7
asserts that the morphism
\nabla_{g,n}
is zero for
n=0
.
But this is the content of
[
28
, Theorem 1.2]
.
∎

8.3.
Proof of Corollary
1.11

To show Corollary
1.11
first note that

\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{k}(\mathrm{Feyn}(H^{\bullet}(\bar{\mathcal{M}}))(\!(g,0)\!))\cong\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{k}_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{g})\cong\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8}H^{6g-6-k}(\mathcal{M}_{g})^{*},

see for example the introduction of
[
28
]
.
The last statement of Proposition
1.7
then states that

\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8}H^{6g-6-k}(\mathcal{M}_{g})=\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{-k}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,0)\!))\oplus\mathrm{gr}_{2}H^{1-k}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g-1,0)\!)).

From part (3) of Corollary
1.8
we then conclude that

\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8}H^{6g-6-k}(\mathcal{M}_{g})\cong\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8}H^{6g-6-k}(\mathrm{HMod}_{g,0}^{0})\oplus\mathrm{gr}_{6g-14}H^{6g-11-k}(\mathrm{HMod}_{g-1,0}^{0})

as desired.

9.
Euler characteristics

We finish this article by considering the weight-graded Euler characteristics of

\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})
,
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})
,
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})
and
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})
.

For a permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
, we define a
symmetric polynomial
p^{\sigma}\in\Lambda_{n}=\mathbb{Q}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}
given by

p^{\sigma}=p_{\lambda_{1}}\cdots p_{\lambda_{\ell}}
,
where
p_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{k}

is the
k
-th power sum symmetric polynomial and
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell})
is the
cycle type
of
\sigma
.
To an
S_{n}
-module
V
on which
\sigma\in S_{n}
acts as
\rho(\sigma)\in\mathrm{End}(V)
we may associate the
Frobenius characteristic
defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}(V)=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\mathrm{tr}(\rho(\sigma))p^{\sigma}.

If
V
is a differential graded
S_{n}
-module, recall that
V^{k}
denotes the degree
k
part of
V
.
The
equivariant Euler characteristic
of
V
is the following symmetric polynomial in
\Lambda_{n}

\chi^{S_{n}}(V)=\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{ch}(V^{k}),

which is a homotopy invariant of
V
.
In case there is an additional weight grading defined on
V
we set

\chi_{t}^{S_{n}}(V):=\sum_{W\geq 0}t^{W}\chi^{S_{n}}(\mathrm{gr}_{W}V)\in\Lambda_{n}[\![t]\!].

Let
\Lambda
be the
ring of symmetric functions
over the rationals. We may define it as the power series ring
\Lambda=\mathbb{Q}[\![p_{1},p_{2},\ldots]\!]
generated by symbols
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots
of degree
1,2,\ldots
respectively, where
p_{k}
stands in for the power symmetric functions of degree
k
.
The
Euler-Frobenius-Poincaré characteristic
of a dg symmetric sequence
\mathcal{P}
with a weight grading is the following power series in
\Lambda[\![t]\!]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathcal{P})=\sum_{n\geq 0}\chi^{S_{n}}_{t}(\mathrm{gr}_{W}\mathcal{P}(\!(n)\!)).

The

Necklace polynomial
is defined as

M_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{d|n}\mu(n/d)t^{d}
, where

\mu
is the number-theoretical Möbius function.

Proposition 9.1
.

The (cyclic) Euler-Frobenius-Poincaré characteristics of
\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}}
,
D\mathsf{BV}^{*}
,
\mathsf{HyCom}
and
\mathsf{BV}
are

(47)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{t^{2}(1-t^{4})}\left((1+p_{1})\prod_{\ell\geq 1}(1+p_{\ell})^{M_{\ell}(t^{2})}-1-(1+t^{2})p_{1}\right)+\frac{1}{1-t^{2}}\frac{p_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{1+t^{2}}\frac{p_{2}}{2}

(48)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})-\frac{t^{2}}{1-t^{2}}\frac{p_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{t^{2}}{1+t^{2}}\frac{p_{2}}{2}

(49)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathsf{HyCom})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})(\!(0,-)\!))

(50)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathsf{BV})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-t^{4}}(1+t^{2}(1-t^{2})p_{1})\left(\prod_{\ell\geq 1}(1+t^{2\ell}(1-t^{2\ell})p_{\ell})^{M_{\ell}(t^{-2})}-1\right)-p_{1}.

Proof.

The formula for
\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})
is due to Getzler
[
17
, Theorem 5.7]
, and has just been adapted to our grading conventions.

For the second formula note that we have

\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})=\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(D^{\prime}\mathsf{BV}^{*})+\chi_{t}^{S_{2}}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(2)\!))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Thm.~\ref{thm:DCV}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})+\chi_{t}^{S_{2}}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(2)\!)).

The weight
W=2k
-part of
D\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(2)\!)
is one-dimensional and spanned by
x_{k}:=\underbrace{\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}}_{k\times}
in degree
2k-1
. Let
\tau\in S_{2}
be the transposition. It acts trivially on
\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}
, but reverses the order of the
\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}
’s and the
k-1
odd compositions so that

\tau\cdot x_{k}=(-1)^{k(k-1)/2}(-1)^{(k-1)(k-2)/2}x_{k}=-(-1)^{k}x_{k}.

Hence we get

\chi_{t}^{S_{2}}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*}(\!(2)\!))=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\geq 1}t^{2k}(-p_{1}^{2}+(-1)^{k}p_{2})=-\frac{t^{2}}{1-t^{2}}\frac{p_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{t^{2}}{1+t^{2}}\frac{p_{2}}{2}.

Equation (
49
) follows from Getzler’s result that
D\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}}\simeq\mathsf{HyCom}^{*}
.

Next consider the formula for
\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathsf{BV})
.
For
n\geq 3
note that we have the principal
(S^{1})^{n}
-bundle

(51)

\mathsf{BV}(\!(n)\!)\to\mathcal{M}_{0,n}=\mathsf{BV}(\!(n)\!)/\left((S^{1})^{n}\right).

It is also
S_{n}
-equivariant.
Hence we have that

\chi_{t}^{S_{n}}(\mathsf{BV}(\!(n)\!))=\chi_{t}^{S_{n}}(H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n}))\circ\chi_{t}^{S_{1}}(H_{\bullet}(S^{1})),

where on the right-hand side we use the plethysm product on symmetric functions
[
18
, Section 7.2]
, and we equip all objects with the weight grading by twice the homological degree.
Obviously,

\chi_{t}^{S_{1}}(H_{\bullet}(S^{1}))=(1-t^{2})p_{1}.

Following Getzler, define the cyclic sequence

\mathbf{m}(\!(n)\!)=\begin{cases}H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n})&\text{for $n\geq 3$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

equipped with the weight grading by twice the homological degree.
From the above it then follows that

(52)

\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathsf{BV})=\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathbf{m})\circ((1-t^{2})p_{1})+\chi_{t}^{S_{2}}(\mathsf{BV}(\!(2)\!))=\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathbf{m})\circ((1-t^{2})p_{1})+\frac{1}{2}(1-t^{2})(p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}).

Getzler
[
17
, Theorem 5.7]
showed that

(53)

\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{1-t^{4}}(1+t^{2}p_{1})\left(\prod_{\ell\geq 1}(1+t^{2\ell}p_{\ell})^{M_{\ell}(t^{-2})}-1-(1+t^{2})p_{1}\right)-\frac{1}{1-t^{2}}\frac{p_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{1+t^{2}}\frac{p_{2}}{2}.

Inserting this into (
52
)
we obtain (
50
).

∎

Explicitly, we have the following leading terms, expressed through Schur polynomials:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})

\displaystyle=s_{3}

\displaystyle-s_{2,2}+s_{4}t^{2}+s_{3,1,1}-s_{3,2}t^{2}+s_{5}t^{4}

\displaystyle-s_{2,2,1,1}-s_{3,3}-s_{4,1,1}+(s_{3,2,1}+s_{4,1,1})t^{2}-s_{4,2}t^{4}+s_{6}t^{6}

\displaystyle+s_{3,1,1,1,1}+s_{3,2,1,1}+s_{3,2,2}+s_{4,2,1}+s_{5,2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(-s_{2,2,2,1}-s_{3,2,1,1}-s_{3,3,1}-s_{4,1,1,1}-s_{4,2,1}-s_{4,3}-s_{5,1,1})t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(s_{3,3,1}+s_{4,2,1}+s_{5,1,1})t^{4}-s_{5,2}t^{6}+s_{7}t^{8}+O(x^{8})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathsf{BV})

\displaystyle=s_{2}-s_{2}t^{2}

\displaystyle+s_{3}+(-s_{2,1}-s_{3})t^{2}+(s_{1,1,1}+s_{2,1})t^{4}-s_{1,1,1}t^{6}

\displaystyle+s_{4}+(-s_{2,2}-s_{3,1}-s_{4})t^{2}+(2s_{2,1,1}+s_{2,2}+2s_{3,1})t^{4}+(-s_{1,1,1,1}-3s_{2,1,1}-2s_{3,1})t^{6}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(s_{1,1,1,1}+s_{2,1,1}+s_{2,2}+s_{3,1})t^{8}+(-s_{2,2})t^{10}

\displaystyle+s_{5}+(-s_{3,2}-s_{4,1}-s_{5})t^{2}+(s_{2,2,1}+3s_{3,1,1}+2s_{3,2}+2s_{4,1})t^{4}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(-3s_{2,1,1,1}-3s_{2,2,1}-6s_{3,1,1}-3s_{3,2}-3s_{4,1})t^{6}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(2s_{1,1,1,1,1}+5s_{2,1,1,1}+5s_{2,2,1}+5s_{3,1,1}+5s_{3,2}+3s_{4,1}+s_{5})t^{8}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(-2s_{1,1,1,1,1}-3s_{2,1,1,1}-5s_{2,2,1}-3s_{3,1,1}-4s_{3,2}-2s_{4,1}-s_{5})t^{10}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(s_{2,1,1,1}+2s_{2,2,1}+2s_{3,1,1}+s_{3,2}+s_{4,1})t^{12}+(-s_{3,1,1})t^{14}

\displaystyle+s_{6}+(-s_{4,2}-s_{5,1}-s_{6})t^{2}+(2s_{3,2,1}+s_{3,3}+3s_{4,1,1}+2s_{4,2}+2s_{5,1})t^{4}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(-3s_{2,2,1,1}-s_{2,2,2}-4s_{3,1,1,1}-7s_{3,2,1}-4s_{3,3}-8s_{4,1,1}-4s_{4,2}-3s_{5,1})t^{6}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(5s_{2,1,1,1,1}+8s_{2,2,1,1}+5s_{2,2,2}+11s_{3,1,1,1}+16s_{3,2,1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+5s_{3,3}+10s_{4,1,1}+10s_{4,2}+5s_{5,1}+s_{6})t^{8}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(-2s_{1,1,1,1,1,1}-10s_{2,1,1,1,1}-12s_{2,2,1,1}-10s_{2,2,2}-15s_{3,1,1,1}-23s_{3,2,1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-5s_{3,3}-11s_{4,1,1}-14s_{4,2}-6s_{5,1}-2s_{6})t^{10}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(3s_{1,1,1,1,1,1}+7s_{2,1,1,1,1}+14s_{2,2,1,1}+8s_{2,2,2}+13s_{3,1,1,1}+20s_{3,2,1}+7s_{3,3}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+12s_{4,1,1}+10s_{4,2}+5s_{5,1}+s_{6})t^{12}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(-s_{1,1,1,1,1,1}-3s_{2,1,1,1,1}-9s_{2,2,1,1}-2s_{2,2,2}-7s_{3,1,1,1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-12s_{3,2,1}-5s_{3,3}-8s_{4,1,1}-4s_{4,2}-3s_{5,1})t^{14}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(s_{2,1,1,1,1}+2s_{2,2,1,1}+s_{2,2,2}+3s_{3,1,1,1}+4s_{3,2,1}+s_{3,3}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+2s_{4,1,1}+2s_{4,2}+s_{5,1})t^{16}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(-s_{2,2,2}-s_{3,1,1,1}-s_{4,2})t^{18}+O(x^{7})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathsf{HyCom})

\displaystyle=s_{3}

\displaystyle+s_{4}+s_{4}t^{2}

\displaystyle+s_{5}+(s_{4,1}+s_{5})t^{2}+s_{5}t^{4}

\displaystyle+s_{6}+(s_{4,2}+s_{5,1}+2s_{6})t^{2}+(s_{4,2}+s_{5,1}+2s_{6})t^{4}+s_{6}t^{6}

\displaystyle+s_{7}+(s_{4,3}+s_{5,2}+2s_{6,1}+2s_{7})t^{2}+(s_{4,2,1}+2s_{4,3}+3s_{5,2}+3s_{6,1}+4s_{7})t^{4}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(s_{4,3}+s_{5,2}+2s_{6,1}+2s_{7})t^{6}+s_{7}t^{8}

\displaystyle+s_{8}+(s_{4,4}+s_{5,3}+2s_{6,2}+2s_{7,1}+3s_{8})t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(s_{4,2,2}+2s_{4,3,1}+3s_{4,4}+2s_{5,2,1}+5s_{5,3}+s_{6,1,1}+7s_{6,2}+6s_{7,1}+6s_{8})t^{4}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(s_{4,2,2}+2s_{4,3,1}+3s_{4,4}+2s_{5,2,1}+5s_{5,3}+s_{6,1,1}+7s_{6,2}+6s_{7,1}+6s_{8})t^{6}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(s_{4,4}+s_{5,3}+2s_{6,2}+2s_{7,1}+3s_{8})t^{8}+s_{8}t^{10}+O(x^{9})

There are no known closed formulas for the Euler characteristics of the Feynman transforms of the above cyclic operads.
However, in low weights closed formulas can sometimes be obtained.
For example, since
\mathrm{gr}_{0}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})\cong\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})
we know the Euler characteristic of
\mathrm{gr}_{0}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})
by the results of
[
7
,
4
]
.
Furthermore, the computation of
[
29
]
can be slightly adapted to yield the following result for the weight 2 part:

Proposition 9.2
.

The equivariant Euler characteristic of
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})
satisfies

(54)

\displaystyle\sum_{g,n}u^{g+n}\chi^{S_{n}}(\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n)\!))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}uZ_{1}\left[\left(-\frac{1}{Z_{1}}+\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\mu(\ell)}{\ell}\left(\log(\ell u^{\ell}Z_{\ell})+\psi_{0}(-Z_{\ell})\right)\right)^{2}+\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\mu(\ell)}{\ell}\left(\log(2\ell u^{2\ell}Z_{2\ell})+\psi_{0}(-Z_{2\ell})\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{1}{(Z_{1})^{2}}+\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\mu(\ell)^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\psi_{1}(-Z_{\ell})\right]

with

Z_{\ell}:=\frac{1}{\ell}\sum_{d\mid\ell}\mu(\ell/d)(u^{-d}+p_{d}))

and

(55)

\psi_{0}(z):=-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{B_{j}}{j}\frac{1}{(-z)^{j}}\mbox{ \ \ \ and \ \ \ }\psi_{1}(z):=-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}B_{j}\frac{1}{(-z)^{j+1}},

where
B_{j}
are the Bernoulli numbers.

Proof sketch.

First note that in the “blown-up” viewpoint, see Section
7.4
, the complex
\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n)\!)
is a complex of graphs of genus
g
with external hairs, exactly two of which are labelled by
\omega
,
n
are numbered and possibly some are labelled by
\epsilon
.
The Euler characteristic of virtually the same graded vector space of graphs has been computed in
[
29
, Section 4.3]
, there denoted
\widetilde{fX}^{\mathit{conn},2}
.
The only differences are that (i) in loc. cit. numbered hairs are considered edges and carry degree 1 and (ii) internal vertices in loc. cit. may not carry tadpoles.
Difference (i) amounts to multiplying the
S_{n}
-representation
\mathrm{Feyn}(\!(g,n)\!)
by the sign representation in degree
n
.
This change is accounted for on the characters by replacing
p_{k}\to-p_{k}
.
Difference (ii) can be accounted for by replacing
\mathsf{BV}_{0}
by
\mathsf{Pois}_{0}
in Proposition 3.2 of loc. cit.
By section 2.4 of loc. cit. the effect is to replace the terms
(1-u^{\ell})u^{\ell}p_{\ell}
in the formula of loc. cit. by
u^{\ell}p_{\ell}
.
Thus we obtain our (
54
).
∎

One may expand the above generating function on the computer.
The result for low
(g,n)
is displayed in Table
9
.
Furthermore, setting
p_{k}=0
for all
k
we obtain

\sum_{g}u^{g}\chi(\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,0)\!))=u-u^{8}+3u^{9}-u^{10}+3u^{11}-4u^{12}+4u^{13}-12u^{14}-2u^{15}-12u^{16}\\
+16u^{17}+33u^{18}+127u^{19}+115u^{20}-267u^{21}-463u^{22}+1718u^{23}-2586u^{24}-45721u^{25}+4560u^{26}\\
+742210u^{27}+65910u^{28}-13604215u^{29}-10317u^{30}+O\left(u^{31}\right)

By Theorem
1.5
and an easy computation in
(g,n)=(1,1)

we then find that

\chi^{S_{n}}(\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!))=\begin{cases}\chi^{S_{n}}(\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n)\!))&\text{for $(g,n)\neq(1,1)$}\\
0&\text{for $(g,n)=(1,1)$}\end{cases}.

Finally, by Theorem
1.3
and a small computation in
(g,n)=(1,0),(1,1),(0,2)
we find that

\chi^{S_{n}}\left(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-8+2n}\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)\right)=\begin{cases}\chi^{S_{n}}(\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n)\!))&\text{for $(g,n)\neq(1,1),(1,0),(0,2)$}\\
p_{1}&\text{for $(g,n)=(1,1)$}\end{cases}.

If sufficiently many terms of the Euler–Frobenius–Poincaré characteristic,
\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathcal{C})
, of a cyclic (co)-operad are known, then we can use the procedure laid out in
[
18
, Section 8]
to compute the Euler characteristic of
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathcal{C})
or
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathcal{C})
. We used an adapted version of the program published with
[
5
]
to compute the Euler characteristics of discussed Feynman transforms in arity
0
up to a couple of orders in genus and in weight.

The weight-graded Euler characteristics of
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!)
,
which by Theorem
1.1
agree with the weight-graded Euler characteristics of the handlebody group,
\mathrm{HMod}_{g,0}^{0}
,
are listed in Table
2
. The top-weight contribution,
which by Theorem
1.2
agrees with the Euler characteristic of

\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})
,
is colored in blue. By Theorem
1.3
the top
-2
-weight contribution is given by
\chi(\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,0)\!))=\chi(\mathrm{gr}_{2}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,0)\!))
, which itself can be computed explicitly as discussed above. The
0
-weight Euler characteristic agrees, by Theorem
1.2
, with the Euler characteristic of
\mathsf{Out}(F_{g})
(see
[
5
]
for a listing of
\chi(\mathsf{Out}(F_{g}))
).

Similarly, the weight-graded Euler characteristics of
\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}})

are listed in Table
3
.
The
0
-weight Euler characteristic agrees with the Euler characteristic of
\mathsf{Out}(F_{g})
as
\mathrm{gr}_{0}\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}}\cong D\mathsf{Com}^{*}
and
H(\mathrm{Feyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D\mathsf{Com}^{*})(\!(g,0)\!))=H(\mathsf{Out}(F_{g}))
.
The Euler characteristic is also known in the top and the
top
-2
-weight by Theorem
4.2
.
See Remark
4.4
for a formula for the entries in weight top
-2
.

Tables
4
and
5
list the
weight-graded
Euler characteristics of
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})
and
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})
respectively.
In both cases, the weight
0
part is equal to the Euler characteristic of

\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{Com})
, because
\mathrm{gr}_{0}(\overline{\mathsf{BV}})=\mathrm{gr}_{0}(\mathsf{HyCom})=\mathsf{Com}
.
The argument for Lemma
4.1
can also be
applied to
\mathsf{HyCom}
. It follows that
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{HyCom})(\!(g,n)\!)
is concentrated in even weights
0,\dots,4g-6+2n
.
This naive top-weight bound is indicated in blue in Table
5
.
However, in the
g=0
case, the contributions to the top weight
vanish due to the symmetries of tadpole graphs.
The top
-2
weight Euler characteristic also appears to vanish.

The auxiliary files to the arXiv version of this article contain
larger versions of Tables
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
with the filenames
tab_DBV.csv
,
tab_Grav.csv
,
tab_BV.csv
and
tab_HyCom.csv
respectively.
These files can be opened with standard spreadsheet software.

In fact, we note that strictly speaking one of the Tables
2
and
4
is redundant, due to the following general fact.

Proposition 9.3
.

For all
(g,n)\neq(0,1)
and all
W
we have that

\chi^{S_{n}}\left(\mathrm{gr}_{W}\mathrm{AFeyn}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)\right)=\chi^{S_{n}}\left(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+2n-W}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n)\!)\right).

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{m}(\!(n)\!)=H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n})
be as in the proof of Proposition
9.1
. Define the auxiliary cyclic pseudo-operad
\mathbf{m}_{S}
such that

\mathbf{m}_{S}(\!(n)\!)=\begin{cases}H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n}\times(S^{1})^{n})=\mathbf{m}(\!(n)\!)\otimes H_{\bullet}(S^{1})^{\otimes n}&\text{for $n\geq 3$}\\
\bar{H}_{\bullet}(S_{1})=\mathbb{Q}\Delta&\text{for $n=2$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

We equip
\mathbf{m}_{S}
with the almost trivial cyclic pseudo-operad structure, defined such that the compositions with the binary operations are defined through the natural action of
\bar{H}_{\bullet}(S_{1})
on
H_{\bullet}(S^{1})
, and all other compositions vanish.
We equip
\mathbf{m}_{S}
with the weight grading by twice the homological degree.
Recall that
\overline{\mathsf{BV}}

is the augmentation ideal of the cyclic operad
\mathsf{BV}
.
From the proof of Proposition
9.1
we see that

\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\mathbf{m}_{S})=\mathrm{Ch}_{t}(\overline{\mathsf{BV}}).

Indeed, the Euler characteristic “cannot see” the nontriviality of the bundle (
51
).
Hence we also have that

\chi^{S_{n}}\left(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6-W}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n)\!)\right)=\chi^{S_{n}}\left(\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+2n-W}\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathbf{m}_{S})(\!(g,n)\!)\right).

We are going to show that the right-hand side is equal to
\chi^{S_{n}}\left(\mathrm{gr}_{W}\mathrm{AFeyn}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)\right)
.
To this end we compute
H(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathbf{m}_{S}))
explicitly.
The graphs spanning the complex
\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathbf{m}_{S})
can have vertices of valences
\geq 2
.
Any such graph we may think of as a
\geq 3
-valent graph, to which strings of bivalent vertices have been added along its edges.

\scriptscriptstyle\Delta
\scriptscriptstyle\Delta
\scriptscriptstyle\Delta
1
2
3

Here we can assume w.l.o.g. that all the bivalent vertices are decorated by
\Delta
, whereas each
\geq 3
-valent vertex
v
is decorated by some element of

\mathbf{m}_{S}(\!(n_{v})\!)=\mathbf{m}(\!(n_{v})\!)\otimes H_{\bullet}(S^{1})^{\otimes n_{v}}.

But for each internal edge in the
\geq 3
-valent graph, the two factors of
H_{\bullet}(S^{1})
from the decorations on the endpoints of the edge and all possible additions of bivalent vertices on the edge yield, up to degree shift, one copy of the two-sided bar construction

B_{2}:=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}H_{\bullet}(S^{1})\otimes(\bar{H}_{\bullet}(S_{1})[1])^{\otimes k}\otimes H_{\bullet}(S_{1}).

Similarly, the contributions of the two-valent vertices on the legs can be combined into a one-sided bar construction

B_{1}:=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}H_{\bullet}(S^{1})\otimes(\bar{H}_{\bullet}(S_{1})[1])^{\otimes k}.

It is well-known that

\displaystyle H(B_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}\Delta_{s}

and

\displaystyle H(B_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q},

with

\Delta_{s}=1\otimes\Delta+\Delta\otimes 1.

From these observations

H(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathbf{m}_{S}))

can be seen as generated by
\mathbf{m}
-decorated graphs with two types of internal edges: One corresponds to the normal edges used in the Feynman transform, of degree
-1
and weight 0.
The other type of edge is of degree
-2
and weight
+2
, and represents the decoration with
\Delta_{s}
.

On the other hand, we have seen in Lemma
3.5
that for
(g,n)\neq(1,0)

H(\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!))=H(\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)).

Furthermore, we have that

\chi^{S_{n}}\left(\mathrm{gr}_{W}\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)\right)=\chi^{S_{n}}\left(\mathrm{gr}_{W}\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(H(D\mathsf{BV}^{*}))(\!(g,n)\!)\right).

Hence it suffices to check that there is an
S_{n}
-equivariant isomorphism of vector spaces

(56)

\phi\colon:\mathrm{gr}_{W}H^{k}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathbf{m}_{S})(\!(g,n)\!))\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+2n-W}\mathrm{AFeyn}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\prime}(H(D\mathsf{BV}^{*}))(\!(g,n)\!)^{6g-6+2n+k}.

In fact, the vector spaces on both sides are generated by the same types of graphs, namely genus
g
graphs with
n
legs and two types of edges. The vertices are either decorated by
\mathbf{m}
or
\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}}
.
Let
\Gamma
be such an
\mathbf{m}
-decorated graph on the left-hand side of (
56
).
Then we define
\phi
by sending
\Gamma
to the graph
\phi(\Gamma)=\tilde{\Gamma}
on the right-hand side of (
56
), built from
\Gamma
by the following replacements.

•

Every decoration by
\mathbf{m}(\!(n_{v})\!)^{-k_{v}}\cong H_{k_{v}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n_{v}})
on some vertex
v
of
\Gamma
is replaced by a decoration in
\widetilde{\mathsf{Grav}}(\!(n_{v})\!)^{2n_{v}-6-k_{v}}\cong H_{c}^{2n_{v}-6-k_{v}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n_{v}})
on the corresponding vertex of
\tilde{\Gamma}
, using the identification

H_{k_{v}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n_{v}})\cong H_{c}^{2n_{v}-6-k_{v}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,n_{v}}).

•

Every “normal” edge in
\Gamma
of degree
-1
and weight 0 is replaced by a marked (
\mathbf{\Delta^{*}}
-decorated) edge of degree
+1
and weight
2
in
\tilde{\Gamma}
.

•

Every
\Delta_{s}
-edge in
\Gamma
of degree
-2
and weight
0
is replaced by a normal edge, of degree and weight 0 in
\tilde{\Gamma}
.

It is clear that the map above defines a bijection on basis elements. We hence just need to verify the degree and weight counts.
Suppose that
\Gamma
has
V
vertices,
e_{1}
normal edges and
e_{2}
marked (
\Delta_{s}
-)edges.
Then it has cohomological degree and weight

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle=-e_{0}-2e_{1}-\sum_{v}k_{v}\text{ and}

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=2e_{1}+2\sum_{v}k_{v}.

On the other hand, the image
\tilde{\Gamma}
of
\Gamma
has degree and weight

\displaystyle\tilde{k}

\displaystyle=e_{0}+\sum_{v}(2n_{v}-6-k_{v})=e_{0}+2n+4(e_{0}+e_{1})-6V-\sum_{v}k_{v}=k+2n+6g-6\text{ and}

\displaystyle\tilde{W}

\displaystyle=2e_{0}+\sum_{v}(2n_{v}-6-2k_{v})=6g-6+2n-W,

as desired.
∎

One can ask to what extent the identification of Proposition
9.3
holds not only on the level of Euler characteristics, but also on the level of the cohomology, and more specifically whether generally

\mathrm{gr}_{W}H^{-k}(\mathrm{Feyn}(\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n)\!))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{gr}_{6g-6+2n-W}H^{6g-6+2n-k}(\mathrm{AFeyn}(D\mathsf{BV}^{*})(\!(g,n)\!)).

Our main results imply that this identification of cohomology holds at least for
W=0,2
. The general case we have to leave to future work.

Appendix A
Giansiracusa’s result for handlebodies with marked points

We note that Theorem
1.1
is essentially shown by Giansiracusa
[
20
]
, but he only considers the case of handlebodies with marked disks, i.e.,
\mathrm{HMod}_{g,0}^{m}
.
We sketch here how to extract the case of handlebodies with marked points from Giansiracusa’s result.

Generally, let
\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}
be the handlebody modular operad, so that

\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}(\!(g,m)\!)\simeq B\mathrm{HMod}_{g,0}^{m},

see
[
20
, Section 4.3]
.

First, we recall the following main result of loc. cit.

Theorem A.1

(Theorem A and Proposition 4.3.2 of
[
20
]
)
.

The handlebody modular operad is weakly equivalent to the homotopy modular closure
\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}_{0}
of its genus zero part
\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}_{0}
for all
(g,n)\neq(1,0)
.
Furthermore, the cyclic operad
\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}_{0}
is homotopy equivalent to the framed little 2-disks operad.

Next, the following relation between the handlebody groups seems to be well known, though we have not found a citeable reference in the literature.

Lemma A.2
.

We have that

B\mathrm{HMod}_{g,n}^{m}\simeq\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}(\!(0,2)\!)^{n}),

where
\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}(\!(0,2)\!)\simeq S^{1}
acts by composition at
n
of the
n+m
marked disks.

Proof.

There is a short exact sequence of groups

(57)

1\to\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathrm{HMod}_{g,0}^{n+m}\to\mathrm{HMod}_{g,n}^{m}\to 1,

with the
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
corresponding to Dehn twists around
n
of the marked disks
(
[
22
, Corollary 5.9 and Corollary 6.2]
, see also
[
21
, Proof of Corollary 3.7]
).

Each such Dehn twist
\phi
can be obtained as follows:
Take
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{2}
a genus zero handlebody with two marked disks. Its handlebody group
\mathrm{HMod}_{0,0}^{2}
is infinite cyclic, generated by a meridional Dehn twist
\phi^{\prime}
(with the definition as in
[
22
, Example 5.1]
).
Now attach one of the two marked disks of
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{2}
to the corresponding marked disk of the original genus
g
handlebody with
n+m
marked disks. The result is again a genus
g
handlebody with
n+m
marked disks and the image of
\phi^{\prime}
in its mapping class group is exactly the Dehn twist
\phi
. This gives an identification of
\mathrm{HMod}_{0,0}^{2}
with the corresponding copy of
\mathbb{Z}
in the kernel of (
57
).

Using the Borel Construction, the short exact sequence of groups in (
57
) gives rise to a map of classifying spaces

B\mathrm{HMod}_{g,0}^{n+m}\to B\mathrm{HMod}_{g,n}^{m},

(a stack in the sense of
[
16
]
)
all of whose fibres are given by a model for
B(\mathrm{HMod}_{0,0}^{2})^{n}\cong B\mathbb{Z}^{n}
(see
[
16
, Theorem 7.1.10]
).
This implies the claim.
∎

Sketch of proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Let
g
,
n
,
m
be such that
2g+n+m\geq 3
.
By Lemma
A.2
we have that

H_{\bullet}(B\mathrm{HMod}_{g,n}^{m})\cong H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}(\!(0,2)\!)^{n})).

Since
(g,n+m)\neq(1,0)
we can apply Theorem
A.1
to obtain

H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}(\!(0,2)\!)^{n}))\cong H_{\bullet}(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}_{0}(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}_{0}(\!(0,2)\!)^{n})).

Arguing similarly to
[
20
, Section 7.1]
it follows from formality of the framed little disks operad that

\displaystyle H_{\bullet}(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}_{0}(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}_{0}(\!(0,2)\!)^{n}))

\displaystyle\cong H_{\bullet}(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}_{0})(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}\mathsf{bdy}_{0})(\!(0,2)\!)^{\otimes n}))

\displaystyle=H_{\bullet}(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}\mathsf{BV}(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}\mathsf{BV}(\!(0,2)\!)^{n})).

However, the homotopy modular closure is computed by the Feynman transform of the dg dual, see
[
33
, Corollary 1.3, 1.4]
, and hence

\displaystyle H_{\bullet}\left(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}\mathsf{BV}(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathbb{L}\mathrm{Mod}_{!}\mathsf{BV}(\!(0,2)\!)^{\otimes n})\right)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle H_{\bullet}\left(\mathrm{Feyn}(D\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathrm{Feyn}(D\mathsf{BV})(\!(0,2)\!)^{\otimes n})\right).

Finally,
\mathrm{Feyn}(D\mathsf{BV})
is a quasi-free modular operad, and in particular a quasi-free
\mathrm{Feyn}(D\mathsf{BV})(\!(0,2)\!)^{\otimes n})
-module, using that
2g+n+m\geq 3
.
Hence the homotopy quotient can be replaced by an ordinary quotient.

\displaystyle H_{\bullet}\left(\mathrm{Feyn}(D\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n+m)\!)/\!/(\mathrm{Feyn}(D\mathsf{BV})(\!(0,2)\!)^{\otimes n})\right)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle H_{\bullet}\left(\mathrm{Feyn}(D\mathsf{BV})(\!(g,n+m)\!)/(\mathrm{Feyn}(D\mathsf{BV})(\!(0,2)\!)^{\otimes n})\right).

But in the special case
m=0
this is just the definition of the amputated Feynman transform of
D\mathsf{BV}
.
∎
